# Joint parameters estimation in cubic tensor model

**arXiv ID**: 2607.29619v1
**Authors**: Sumit Mukherjee, Arnab Sen, Qiang Wu
**Published**: 2026-07-31
**Categories**: math.ST, math.PR
**Comments**: 52 pages, 0 figure
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.29619v1

## Abstract

We study joint parameter estimation from a single observation in high-dimensional Gibbs measures with cubic tensor interactions, motivated by dense ERGMs, arithmetic-progression models, and inhomogeneous random hypergraphs. Focusing on the maximum pseudolikelihood estimator, we give checkable conditions for joint consistency and asymptotic ill-conditioning. For the edge-triangle ERGM, pseudolikelihood is ill-conditioned in the ferromagnetic regime with nonnegative field, but consistent in a sufficiently strong antiferromagnetic regime. For the edge-three-star ERGM, it is ill-conditioned for all inverse temperatures and external fields. We also study consistency for arithmetic-progression, and inhomogeneous hypergraph models. Our proofs develop nonlinear large-deviation and mean-field approximation tools for cubic tensor Gibbs measures, which have scope for broad applications.

## Full Text

Joint Parameters Estimation in Cubic Tensor Model

## Joint Parameters Estimation in Cubic Tensor ModelSumit Mukherjee⋆,Arnab Sen†andQiang Wu‡⋆Department of Statistics, Columbia University, 1255 Amsterdam Ave
New York, New York 10027

neapolis, MN 55455.⋆sm3949@columbia.edu,†arnab@umn.edu,‡wuq@umn.edu.

## Abstract.

We study joint parameter estimation from a single observation in high-dimensional Gibbs measures with cubic tensor interactions, motivated by dense ERGMs, arithmetic-progression models, and inhomogeneous random hypergraphs. Focusing on the maximum pseudolikelihood estimator, we give checkable conditions for joint consistency and asymptotic ill-conditioning. For the edge–triangle ERGM, pseudolikelihood is ill-conditioned in the ferromagnetic regime with nonnegative field, but consistent in a sufficiently strong antiferromagnetic regime. For the edge–three-star ERGM, it is ill-conditioned for all inverse temperatures and external fields. We also study consistency for arithmetic-progression, and inhomogeneous hypergraph models. Our proofs develop nonlinear large-deviation and mean-field approximation tools for cubic tensor Gibbs measures, which have scope for broad applications.

## Key words and phrases:exponential random graph models, pseudolikelihood estimation, Gibbs measures, cubic tensor models, mean-field approximation

## 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification:Primary 62F12; secondary 60F10

## 1.Introduction and Main Results

In this paper we study joint estimation of parameters in a class of Gibbs measures, where one of the underlying sufficient statistics is a cubic tensor. One important class of examples of such cubic tensors models come from the so called exponential random graph models of social networks or ERGMs (see[holland1981exponential,snijders2006new,wasserman1994social,lusher2013exponential]). In particular if the motif on the ERGM is a triangle or a three-star, then the corresponding tensor is cubic(2.1).
Another class of examples of tensors forms arising from combinatorics and probability is the number of 3-Arithmetic Progressions in a random subset of[n]:={1,2,⋯,n}[n]:=\{1,2,\cdots,n\}(see2.2). Large deviations for the number of 3-APs in a random subset has been studied recently in probability, using the framework of non-linear large deviations[CD16], with sharper upper-tail estimates obtained in[BhattacharyaGangulyShaoZhao20,Warnke17].
A natural third class of examples comes from inhomogeneous random hypergraphs, which extend inhomogeneous random graphs to higher-order interactions[bollobas2007phase,lovasz2012large]. For more details on this example, we refer the reader to Section2.3.

Prior to this work, there has been a significant recent research focus on parameter estimation for Gibbs measures with quadratic interaction, which focus on the so called Ising and Potts models on a weighted graph, and spin glass models. The pioneering work in this area was done in[Chat07], where the author studies estimation of
a single parameter, and establishesN\sqrt{N}consistency of the pseudo-likelihood, under very mild assumptions on the underlying matrix which controls the quadratic form. In particular, this work allows the matrix to be a (scaled) adjacency graph (which correspond to Ising models), as well as take both positive and negative values simultaneously (which correspond to the so called spin glass models in statistical physics). In a follow up work,[bhattacharya2018inference]considers the same problem of one parameter estimation, and establishes interesting phase transitions in the rate of consistency of the pseudo-likelihood estimator. The problem of joint parameter estimation for Ising models is much more difficult task and exhibit more delicate behavior, this was first studied in[GM20], where the authors give natural sufficient conditions forN\sqrt{N}-consistency of both the parameters simultaneously. However, the results of[GM20]do not cover spin glass models. Recently, in[CSW24]the authors establish joint consistency for quadratic spin glass models using a small ball probability argument. In the more recent work[mukherjee2026joint], the authors establish sufficient conditions forN\sqrt{N}-consistency for Potts models, which is an extension of Ising models to multiple colors. To the best of our knowledge, a rigorous study of joint parameter estimation for cubic and higher order tensors has not been carried out in the literature. The aim of the current work is to address this gap, by studying joint estimation of parameters in the cubic tensor case.

## 1.1.Our Contributions

We summarize the main contributions of this paper. We introduce a general framework for joint parameter estimation in Gibbs measures with cubic tensor interactions. This framework is motivated by several concrete models spanning in different fields, including the edge-triangle and edge-three-star ERGM, Gibbs measures tilted by three-term arithmetic progression counts, and inhomogeneous random hypergraph models.

Our first main result Theorem1.3identifies the main statistical mechanism behind joint pseudolikelihood estimation. Roughly speaking, the joint maximum pseudolikelihood estimator is consistent with an explicit quantitative error bound when the conditional local statistics exhibit enough variability across coordinates. Conversely, when the local statistics become nearly homogeneous, the pseudolikelihood estimator becomes ill-conditioned and estimation might be impossible.

Building on this, Theorem1.4gives general and easily checkable structural conditions under which joint estimation succeeds. These conditions are expressed directly in terms of the interaction tensor, and hence can be verified in concrete examples. A key message is that structural inhomogeneity of the tensor can create enough variation in the conditional local statistics to make joint estimation possible. Under these conditions, the maximum pseudolikelihood estimator is jointlyN\sqrt{N}-consistent.
As a complement, Theorem1.13identifies a broad homogeneous ferromagnetic regime in which pseudolikelihood becomes asymptotically ill-conditioned. This result gives a rigorous explanation for the difficulty of joint estimation in dense ERGM-type models: in homogeneous ferromagnetic regimes, the model may behave as if it has only one effective field, making the linear and cubic parameters hard to distinguish through pseudolikelihood.

Along the way to prove Theorem1.4and1.13, we develop a mean-field theory for cubic tensor Gibbs measures in Theorems1.10and1.11. In particular, Theorem1.10provides a variational approximation for the free energy and a low-complexity description of the conditional mean vectors. Theorem1.11then shows that if the associated variational problem is separated from nearly constant profiles, the local statistics remain sufficiently inhomogeneous and jointN\sqrt{N}-consistent estimation follows. Since mean-field approximation has broad applications in the study of high-dimensional probability and statistics, we expect these results to be useful beyond the estimation problems considered in this paper.

Finally, Section2applies the general theory to the three families of examples. For the edge-triangle ERGM, we show that pseudolikelihood estimation is ill-conditioned in the ferromagnetic regime with nonnegative field, but that joint estimation becomes possible in a sufficiently strong antiferromagnetic regime. This is in contrast to what happens for the edge-three-star ERGM, where we show that the pseudolikelihood estimation remains ill-conditioned in both ferromagnetic and anti ferromagnetic regimes. For three-term arithmetic progression models, we obtain different conclusions in the cyclic and integer settings, reflecting the different homogeneity properties of the two tensors. For inhomogeneous random hypergraph models, we show that non-constant vertex profiles lead to consistent joint estimation, while sufficiently homogeneous ferromagnetic models fall into the ill-conditioned regime.

Suppose we have access to a sampleXXfrom the ground truth distributionPβ0,h0P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}, the goal is to estimate the ground truth(β0,h0)(\beta_{0},h_{0})using the sample. A natural estimator is the maximum likelihood estimate (MLE), obtained by maximizing the log likelihood function. Unfortunately, a major challenge for such models is the intractability of the normalizing constant/partition function, and so the log-likelihood function is hard to work with, both analytically and computationally. To bypass this, the focus is on studying the pseudo-likelihood estimator of Besag[besag1975statistical], computing which does not require the knowledge of the normalizing constant, and is hence numerically feasible.

Before we formally define the pseudo-likelihood estimator, first note that the conditional distribution ofXiX_{i}given(Xj=xj,j≠i)(X_{j}=x_{j},j\neq i)isμβ​mi​(x)+h\mu_{\beta m_{i}(x)+h}which has the meanbi​(x):=Λ′​(β​mi​(x)+h)b_{i}(x):=\Lambda^{\prime}(\beta m_{i}(x)+h)(in the notation of Definition1.1),

## 1.3.mean-field Tensors

A natural question is whetherN\sqrt{N}-consistency fails, when both the sufficient conditions in Theorem1.4fails. In this case the situation is a bit more delicate. To study this, we introduce the following conditions:mean-field condition:∥∑iAi2∥op=o((logN)−1),\displaystyle\textit{mean-field condition}:\quad\big\|\sum_{i}A_{i}^{2}\big\|_{\rm{op}}=o((\log N)^{-1}),(4)asymptotic regularity condition:∑i(ℛi−ℛ¯)2=o(N).\displaystyle\textit{asymptotic regularity condition}:\quad\sum_{i}(\mathcal{R}_{i}-\bar{\mathcal{R}})^{2}=o(N).(5)

## Remark 1.6.

Assumption (4) may not immediately look natural, but it is indeed a slight strengthening of the more natural assumptionTr​(ℛ2)=o​(N/log⁡N){\rm Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})=o(N/\log N)arising from Theorem1.4(see Lemma3.4(a)). The converse implication is false in general. However, Lemma3.4(b)shows that for tensors satisfying the following two conditionsstrong pseudo-regularity condition:maxℛi​j>0ℛi​j⩽Kminℛi​j>0ℛi​j,\displaystyle\textit{strong pseudo-regularity condition}:\quad\max_{\mathcal{R}_{ij}>0}\mathcal{R}_{ij}\leqslant K\min_{\mathcal{R}_{ij}>0}\mathcal{R}_{ij},(6)non degeneracy condition:ℛ¯⩾α>0andℛi>0for alli∈[N].\displaystyle\textit{non degeneracy condition}:\bar{\mathcal{R}}\geqslant\alpha>0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{R}_{i}>0\text{ for all }i\in[N].(7)

the converse does hold. Thus, for tensors satisfying the above two conditions, the mean-field condition is equivalent to the more natural conditionTr​(ℛ2)=o​(N/log⁡N){\rm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})}=o(N/\log N). Condition (6)
essentially demands comparable co-degrees of the underlying hypergraph, whereas condition (7) rules out degenerate cases (such asA≡0A\equiv 0). It turns out that all our examples in Section2satisfy both the above conditions, and so thus mean-field can be checked viaTr​(ℛ2){\rm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})}.
We point out that the strong pseudo-regularity condition only requires thenonzeroentries of the matrixℛ\mathcal{R}to be comparable to one another.
This is weaker than requiring all entries ofℛ\mathcal{R}to be equal or comparable.
In the hypergraph setting, this corresponds to asking that the positive
pair-codegrees are comparable, rather than requiring pair-codegree regularity
over all pairs. This distinction is useful, since many natural examples have
zero pair-codegrees, which includes the edge-triangle and edge-three-star ERGM.

We will now give a structural result for the cubic Gibbs measures, where the underlying tensorAAsatisfies (4). To state this result, we first need to introduce some notations.

Next, we introduce the definition of a sequence of low-complexity sets in[κ−,κ+]N[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}.

## Definition 1.8(Low complexity set).

Let(EN)N⩾1⊆[κ−,κ+]N(E_{N})_{N\geqslant 1}\subseteq[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}be a sequence of sets. We say thatENE_{N}is of low complexity, if for everyδ>0\delta>0there exists aδ​N\delta\sqrt{N}-net𝒟N​(δ)\mathcal{D}_{N}(\delta)for the setENE_{N}in Euclidean metric, such thatlog⁡|𝒟N​(δ)|=o​(N)\log|\mathcal{D}_{N}(\delta)|=o(N).

Finally, we introduce the notion of mean-field cubic Gibbs measures on[κ−,κ+]N[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}, see the similar notions in[basak2017universality,lacker2024mean].

## Definition 1.9(Mean-field measure).

For everyN⩾1N\geqslant 1, letℙN\mathbb{P}_{N}be a probability measure on[κ−,κ+]N[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}. We will say that the sequence of probabilityℙN\mathbb{P}_{N}is mean-field, ifinfℚN∈prod​([κ−,κ+]N)KL​(ℚN∥ℙN)=o​(N).\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{Q}_{N}\in{\rm prod}([\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N})}{\rm{KL}}(\mathbb{Q}_{N}\|\mathbb{P}_{N})=o(N).

In words, the distributionℙN\mathbb{P}_{N}is well approximated in terms of the Kullback-Leibler divergence by product measures. In particular, ifℙN=Pβ,h\mathbb{P}_{N}=P_{\beta,h}from (1), then a straight-forward calculation givesinfℚN∈prod​([κ−,κ+]N)KL​(ℚN∥ℙN)=log⁡ZN​(β,h)−supt∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(t)−I​(t)),\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{Q}_{N}\in{\rm prod}([\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N})}\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{Q}_{N}\|\mathbb{P}_{N})=\log Z_{N}(\beta,h)-\sup_{t\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big(f(t)-I(t)\big),

whereI​(t):=∑iI​(ti)I(t):=\sum_{i}I(t_{i})withI​(ti)I(t_{i})defined in (8).
Consequently, the measurePβ,hP_{\beta,h}is mean-field ifflog⁡ZN​(β,h)−supt∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(t)−I​(t))=o​(N).\displaystyle\log Z_{N}(\beta,h)-\sup_{t\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big(f(t)-I(t)\big)=o(N).(9)

## Theorem 1.10.

Suppose the tensorAAsatisfies the Assumption1.2, and the mean-field condition (4).
- (i)

Then the33-tensor modelPβ,hP_{\beta,h}is mean-field, in the sense that (9) holds.
- (ii)

Moreover, the set of all conditional meansℳN:={b​(x):x∈[κ−,κ+]N}\mathcal{M}_{N}:=\{b(x):x\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}\}has low complexity in the sense of Definition1.8.

We will now show that for mean-field tensors (i.e. when (4) holds), joint estimation using pseudo-likelihood may or may not be possible, depending on the parameter(β0,h0)(\beta_{0},h_{0}). Our first result gives a sufficient condition for jointN\sqrt{N}estimation for mean-field tensors.

## Theorem 1.11.

LetX∼Pβ0,h0X\sim P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}, where the underlying tensorAAsatisfies Assumption1.2and the mean-field condition (4). Suppose that there exist constantsε>0\varepsilon>0andδ>0\delta>0such that, for all sufficiently largeNN,supy∈[κ−,κ+]N∑i(yi−y¯)2⩽ε​N(f​(y)−I​(y))⩽supy∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(y)−I​(y))−δ​N.\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}\\
\sum_{i}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}\leqslant\varepsilon N\end{subarray}}\bigl(f(y)-I(y)\bigr)\leqslant\sup_{y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\bigl(f(y)-I(y)\bigr)-\delta N.

Then there existsc>0c>0such thatPβ0,h0​(TN​(X)⩾c)→1.P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}(T_{N}(X)\geqslant c)\to 1.

Consequently, the joint pseudo-likelihood estimator isN\sqrt{N}-consistent at(β0,h0)(\beta_{0},h_{0}).

Our second result shows that estimation may also be difficult using pseudo-likelihood under certain parameter regimes, under the following “well-connectedness” assumptions on the tensorAA. We first assume that the tensorAAsatisfies (7).
Then the Markov transition matrix𝒫:=D−1​ℛ\mathcal{P}:=D^{-1}\mathcal{R}is well defined, where
whereD=diag​(ℛ1,…,ℛN)D=\mathrm{diag}(\mathcal{R}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{R}_{N}).
Since𝒫=D−1/2​(D−1/2​ℛ​D−1/2)​D1/2\mathcal{P}=D^{-1/2}\big(D^{-1/2}\mathcal{R}D^{-1/2}\big)D^{1/2}is similar to the symmetric matrixD−1/2​ℛ​D−1/2D^{-1/2}\mathcal{R}D^{-1/2}, all its eigenvalues are real and can be listed as1=λ1⩾λ2⩾⋯⩾λN⩾−1.1=\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{N}\geqslant-1.

Finally, we need the following technical definition on the reference measureμ\mu.

## Definition 1.12(Stochastic non-negativity).

The reference measureμ\muis stochastic non-negative ifI​(t)⩽I​(−t)I(t)\leqslant I(-t)fort⩾0t\geqslant 0. Note that this definition impliesκ+⩾|κ−|\kappa_{+}\geqslant\left|\kappa_{-}\right|.

We now give some examples of stochastically non-negative measures. All the implied claims are verified in[BDM23, Proposition 1.3].
- •

Any measure which is supported on non-negative reals is stochastically non-negative. In particular, this includes
Bernoulli(pp), andU​[0,1]U[0,1].
- •

Any symmetric measure is stochastically non-negative. In particular, this includes Rademacher andU​[−1,1]U[-1,1].
- •

More generally, any measure which is a non-negative tilt of a symmetric measure is stochastically non-negative. In particular, this includes asymmetric Rademacher distributionsμ=p​δ−1+(1−p)​δ1\mu=p\delta_{-1}+(1-p)\delta_{1}withp⩽1/2p\leqslant 1/2.

Our next result gives a sufficient condition forTNT_{N}to beop​(1)o_{p}(1)for mean-field regular tensors, suggesting that estimation using pseudo-likelihood can be difficult in certain parameter regimes.

## Theorem 1.13.

Suppose that the tensorAAsatisfies Assumption1.2, as well as conditions (4), (5), (7) and (10). Assume that the reference measureμ\muis stochastically non-negative. LetX∼Pβ0,h0X\sim P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}withβ0>0\beta_{0}>0andh0⩾0h_{0}\geqslant 0. If either (i)h0>0h_{0}>0or (ii)κ−⩾0\kappa_{-}\geqslant 0, thenTN​(X)=op​(1).T_{N}(X)=o_{p}(1).

## Remark 1.14.

In particular, if the tensor is either the complete tensorAi​j​k=1N2​1{i≠j≠k}A_{ijk}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}1_{\{i\neq j\neq k\}}, or a scaled Erdős-Rényi hypergraph with parameterppfixed, then extending the arguments of[GM20, Theorem 1.6]for the quadratic case it should be possible to show that consistent joint estimation of parameters is impossible using any estimator, and not just the pseudo-likelihood estimator. Note that both these tensors are both (approximately) regular (i.e. satisfies (5)) and mean-field (i.e. satisfies (4)). This demonstrates that for certain parameter regimes, estimation can indeed be hard (if not impossible) for mean-field regular tensors. Since this should be a straight-forward extension of the arguments of[GM20, Theorem 1.6], we do not repeat this here.

## Remark 1.15.

We formulate the paper under the global standing assumption thatAi​j​k⩾0A_{ijk}\geqslant 0, since this covers the main examples considered in Section2and
keeps the notation simple. The non-negativity assumption is only essential for Theorem1.13. In contrast, Theorems1.3,1.4,1.10and1.11can be extended to signed
symmetric tensors vanishing on diagonals, provided Assumption1.2is replaced by the absolute row-sum conditionmaxi​∑j,k|Ai​j​k|⩽γ.\max_{i}\sum_{j,k}|A_{ijk}|\leqslant\gamma.In that signed extension, the bounds in the concentration results of
Section3should be in terms of the absolute tensorA†=(|Ai​j​k|)i,j,k∈[N]A^{\dagger}=(|A_{ijk}|)_{i,j,k\in[N]}. We do not state this extra generality separately. An extra caveat for Theorem1.11is that a mean-field type condition forA†A^{\dagger}needs be assumed.

## 1.4.Future Scope

We view this paper as a first step toward a broader understanding of inference in cubic, and more generally higher-order, interaction models. Several natural directions remain open. One important problem is to establish limiting distributions for the maximum pseudolikelihood estimator in regimes where it is consistent. Such results would allow one to construct confidence sets for the unknown parameters. This question is closely related to the study of limiting distributions for conditionally centered sums of spins, or magnetizations, which are themselves objects of independent interest in probability and statistical physics. As is well known, magnetization fluctuations in tensor models may be non-Gaussian, particularly near phase-transition boundaries.
Another natural direction is to incorporate a quadratic interaction term, that is, a matrix-valued tensor, leading to a three-parameter model. Establishing sufficient conditions for estimability in this setting would require controlling local fields generated jointly by the cubic and quadratic tensors. This interaction appears difficult to handle directly with the techniques developed here.
A third direction is to extend our analysis to spin-glass tensors, allowing both positive and negative entries, in the spirit of the quadratic results of[CSW24]. As emphasized there, spin-glass models require different tools, and we expect the corresponding inference theory to exhibit qualitatively different behavior.
Finally, it would be interesting to extend our results to tensors of order higher than three. Some of our arguments seem to admit relatively straightforward extensions, while others appear to require new ideas. Moreover, we expect the behavior of higher-order tensor models to depend substantially on the parity of the interaction order, even versus odd, as has been observed for complete tensors in[mukherjee2021fluctuations].

## 1.5.Structure of the Paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section2discusses applications to the main examples, including the edge-triangle and edge-three-star ERGM,
the 3-term arithmetic progression model, and inhomogeneous random hypergraph models, and
deduces their specific estimation consequences from Theorems1.3,1.4,1.11, and1.13.
Section3collects the preliminary concentration estimates, the mean-field criterion,
the connectivity criterion, and auxiliary lemmas. Section4proves
Theorem1.4. Section5proves
Theorems1.10,1.11, and1.13.
AppendixAproves Theorem1.3, and
AppendixBcontains the proofs of the application results from
Section2. AppendixCcollects the proofs of the auxiliary lemmas in Section3.3.

## 2.Applications

The general cubic tensor model applies to several natural families of examples. In this
section we record three representative applications: edge-triangle and edge-three-star
ERGMs, Gibbs measures tilted by three-term arithmetic progression counts, and
inhomogeneous random hypergraph tensors. The proofs reduce to verifying the structural
hypotheses of the general theorems and are given in AppendixB.

## 2.1.Exponential Random Graph Models

We first consider dense ERGMs whose sufficient statistics are the edge count and a cubic
motif count. ERGMs model network data through exponential-family weights on graph
features such as edge density and local subgraph counts[holland1981exponential,lusher2013exponential,snijders2006new,wasserman1994social].
Dense ERGMs are known to exhibit degeneracy and near Erdős–Rényi behavior in
parts of parameter space[chatterjee2013estimating,schweinberger2011instability], which suggests that joint estimation of the edge and motif parameters can be difficult
in certain parts of the parameter regime. Our results make this precise for
pseudolikelihood: the edge–triangle model is ill-conditioned in the ferromagnetic
nonnegative-field regime but becomes estimable in a sufficiently strong antiferromagnetic
regime, while the edge–three-star model is ill-conditioned for all fixed parameters.

## 2.1.1.Edge-Triangle ERGM

SettingN=(n2)N=\binom{n}{2}, we consider the following probability distribution on the space of edge weights of the complete graphKnK_{n}, identified with the upper-triangular entries of a symmetricn×nn\times nmatrixG=(Gi​j)1⩽i,j⩽nG=(G_{ij})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}withGi​i=0G_{ii}=0andGj​i=Gi​jG_{ji}=G_{ij}:d​Pβ,hd​μ⊗N​(G)=exp⁡(β3​n​∑i,j,kGi​j​Gj​k​Gk​i+h​∑i<jGi​j)ZN​(β,h).\displaystyle\frac{dP_{\beta,h}}{d\mu^{\otimes N}}(G)=\frac{\exp(\frac{\beta}{3n}\sum_{i,j,k}G_{ij}G_{jk}G_{ki}+h\sum_{i<j}G_{ij})}{Z_{N}(\beta,h)}.(11)

This is a special case of the general model (1), with tensorAe0​e1​e2=n−1​1{(e0,e1,e2)​form a triangle},for any edgese0,e1,e2.\displaystyle A_{e_{0}e_{1}e_{2}}=n^{-1}1_{\{(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2})\text{ form a triangle}\}},\qquad\text{for any edges $e_{0},e_{1},e_{2}$}.(12)

In particular, whenμ=12​(δ0+δ1)\mu=\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{0}+\delta_{1}), the configuration space becomes{0,1}N\{0,1\}^{N}, and (11) reduces to the classical edge-triangle exponential random graph model, commonly referred to as ERGM in the statistics and social science literature, see[holland1981exponential,wasserman1994social,snijders2006new].

## Theorem 2.1.

Consider the edge-triangle ERGM defined in (11), letX∼Pβ0,h0X\sim P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}. Then the following holds:
- (a)

Supposeμ\muis stochastically non-negative,β0>0,h0⩾0\beta_{0}>0,h_{0}\geqslant 0. If eitherh0>0h_{0}>0orκ−⩾0\kappa_{-}\geqslant 0, thenTN​(X)=op​(1)T_{N}(X)=o_{p}(1)underPβ0,h0P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}.
- (b)

Supposeκ−=0\kappa_{-}=0andμ​({0})>0\mu(\{0\})>0. For anyh0∈ℝh_{0}\in\mathbb{R}there existsL​(h0)>0L(h_{0})>0, such that for allβ0⩽−L​(h0)\beta_{0}\leqslant-L(h_{0})we havePβ0,h0​(TN>c)→1P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}(T_{N}>c)\to 1, for somec>0c>0depending on(β0,h0,μ)(\beta_{0},h_{0},\mu). Consequently, the maximum pseudolikelihood estimator exists with probability tending to one
and is jointlyN\sqrt{N}-consistent.

## Remark 2.2.

Part (a) of the above theorem suggests a bottleneck for estimation using pseudo-likelihood in this regime. Similar findings have been reported in the literature on ERGMs[winstein2026wasserstein,bhamidi2008mixing,chatterjee2013estimating,eldan2018exponential].
In contrast, part (b) shows that consistent estimation of both parameters is possible in the strong anti-ferromagnetic regime{β0⩽−L,h0∈ℝ}\{\beta_{0}\leqslant-L,\ h_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\}, provided thatL=L​(h0)>0L=L(h_{0})>0is chosen sufficiently large. To the best of our knowledge, this has not been established before, although[chatterjee2013estimating, Theorem 7.1]is a step in that direction.

## 2.1.2.Edge-Three-Star ERGM

We next consider the edge-three-star ERGM. A
three-star consists of three distinct edges sharing a common vertex. As
before, setN=(n2)N=\binom{n}{2}, and identify the space of edge weights ofKnK_{n}with the upper-triangular entries of a symmetricn×nn\times nmatrixG=(Gi​j)1⩽i,j⩽nG=(G_{ij})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}, whereGi​i=0G_{ii}=0andGj​i=Gi​jG_{ji}=G_{ij}. Now the Gibbs measure isd​Pβ,hd​μ⊗N​(G)=ZN​(β,h)−1​exp⁡(β3​n2​∑i,j,k,ℓ​distinctGi​j​Gi​k​Gi​ℓ+h​∑i<jGi​j)\frac{dP_{\beta,h}}{d\mu^{\otimes N}}(G)=Z_{N}(\beta,h)^{-1}\exp\Big(\frac{\beta}{3n^{2}}\sum_{i,j,k,\ell\,{\rm distinct}}G_{ij}G_{ik}G_{i\ell}+h\sum_{i<j}G_{ij}\Big)(13)

This is a special case of the general model (1), with tensorAe0​e1​e2=1n2​𝟏​{(e0,e1,e2)​form a three-star},A_{e_{0}e_{1}e_{2}}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathbf{1}\{(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2})\text{ form a three-star}\},(14)

for any edgese0,e1,e2∈E​(Kn)e_{0},e_{1},e_{2}\in E(K_{n}). Here, the event in
(14) means thate0,e1,e2e_{0},e_{1},e_{2}are distinct and
share a common vertex. In particular, whenμ=12​(δ0+δ1),\mu=\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{0}+\delta_{1}),the configuration space becomes{0,1}N\{0,1\}^{N}, and
(13) reduces, up to the normalization of the three-star
parameter, to the classical edge-three-star ERGM in[chatterjee2013estimating,snijders2006new,wasserman1994social].

## Theorem 2.3.

Consider the edge-three-star ERGM defined in (13), and letX∼Pβ0,h0X\sim P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}. Then for anyβ0,h0∈ℝ\beta_{0},h_{0}\in\mathbb{R}, we haveTN​(X)=op​(1)T_{N}(X)=o_{p}(1)underPβ0,h0P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}.

Both the edge-triangle and edge-three-star ERGMs satisfy the mean-field condition (4), the asymptotic regularity condition (5), and the spectral-gap condition (10), as verified in LemmasB.1andB.3in the Appendix. Despite these common structural properties, the two models have different variational behavior. For the edge-triangle model, Theorem1.13givesTN​(X)=op​(1)T_{N}(X)=o_{p}(1)in the ferromagnetic regime, thus pseudolikelihood estimation is asymptotically ill-conditioned. In the strongly antiferromagnetic regime, however, LemmaB.2shows that near-maximizers of the variational problem remain a positive distance from constant vectors. Theorem1.11then yields jointN\sqrt{N}-consistent estimation. For the edge-three-star model, LemmaB.4shows that near-maximizers are asymptotically close to constant vectors for every fixed(β0,h0)(\beta_{0},h_{0}), in both the ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic regimes. Consequently,TN​(X)=op​(1)T_{N}(X)=o_{p}(1)throughout the parameter space.

## 2.2.Three Terms Arithmetic Progression

Our second application concerns Gibbs measures tilted by the number of nontrivial
three-term arithmetic progressions. The extremal problem of finding large subsets of[N][N]with no nontrivial
3-AP goes back to Roth’s theorem[Roth53], and quantitative refinements remain an
active topic[bourgain1999,kelley2023strong,Sanders11]. Its probabilistic counterpart
is the study of upper tails for 3-AP counts in random subsets, where nonlinear large
deviation methods were developed in[CD16]and sharpened in[BhattacharyaGangulyShaoZhao20,Warnke17].

We consider both the integer and cyclic versions, since they lead to different tensor
geometries. Inℤ\mathbb{Z}, a nontrivial 3-AP is a triple of the form(a,a+r,a+2​r)(a,a+r,a+2r)withr≠0r\neq 0, equivalently a triple(a,b,c)(a,b,c)witha+c=2​ba+c=2banda,b,ca,b,cdistinct. Inℤ/N​ℤ\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}, the same definition is
used moduloNN, again requiring the three entries to be pairwise distinct. For a setS⊂[N]S\subset[N]orS⊂ℤ/N​ℤS\subset\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}, letT3int​(S)T_{3}^{\mathrm{int}}(S)andT3cyc​(S)T_{3}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(S)denote the corresponding numbers
of nontrivial 3-APs contained inSS. Equivalently, a three-point set{a,b,c}\{a,b,c\}contributes if some ordering of its elements forms a nontrivial arithmetic
progression in the relevant ambient group.

We now define the associated Gibbs measures. Forx∈{0,1}Nx\in\{0,1\}^{N}, identifyxxwith
the subset{i:xi=1}\{i:x_{i}=1\}. LetPβ,hintP_{\beta,h}^{\mathrm{int}}andPβ,hcycP_{\beta,h}^{\mathrm{cyc}}be the Gibbs distributions of the form (1),
with reference measureμ=12​δ0+12​δ1\mu=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{1}, obtained from the tensorsAa,b,cint:=1N​𝟏{{a,b,c}​forms a nontrivial 3-AP in​ℤ},Aa,b,ccyc:=1N​𝟏{{a,b,c}​forms a nontrivial 3-AP in​ℤ/N​ℤ}.A^{\mathrm{int}}_{a,b,c}:=\frac{1}{N}\mathbf{1}_{\{\{a,b,c\}\text{ forms a nontrivial 3-AP in }\mathbb{Z}\}},\quad A^{\mathrm{cyc}}_{a,b,c}:=\frac{1}{N}\mathbf{1}_{\{\{a,b,c\}\text{ forms a nontrivial 3-AP in }\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}\}}.

fora,b,c∈[N]a,b,c\in[N]. Thushhcontrols the density of the random set, whileβ\betacontrols the arithmetic interaction: positiveβ\betafavors sets with many
3-APs, whereas negativeβ\betafavors progression-sparse sets. Our results on pseudolikelihood estimation for the33-AP models above are summarized in the following theorem.

## Theorem 2.4.

Consider the 3-AP models defined above withμ=12​δ0+12​δ1\mu=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{1}.
- (i)

In the cyclic caseA=AcycA=A^{\mathrm{cyc}}, the following statements hold.
- (a)

Ifβ0>0\beta_{0}>0andh0⩾0h_{0}\geqslant 0, thenTN​(X)=op​(1)T_{N}(X)=o_{p}(1)underPβ0,h0cycP^{\mathrm{cyc}}_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}.
- (b)

For everyh0∈ℝh_{0}\in\mathbb{R}, there existsL=L​(h0)>0L=L(h_{0})>0such that, wheneverβ0⩽−L\beta_{0}\leqslant-L, there is a constantc>0c>0withPβ0,h0cyc​(TN​(X)⩾c)→1.P^{\mathrm{cyc}}_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}\bigl(T_{N}(X)\geqslant c\bigr)\to 1.Consequently, the MPLE exists with probability tending to one and is jointlyN\sqrt{N}-consistent for(β0,h0)(\beta_{0},h_{0}).
- (ii)

In the integer caseA=AintA=A^{\mathrm{int}}, for every fixed(β0,h0)∈ℝ2(\beta_{0},h_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},TN​(X)=Ωp​(1)T_{N}(X)=\Omega_{p}(1)underPβ0,h0intP^{\mathrm{int}}_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}. Consequently, the MPLE exists with probability tending to one and is jointlyN\sqrt{N}-consistent for(β0,h0)(\beta_{0},h_{0}).

The integer and cyclic models exhibit different behavior. In the integer model, the number of33-APs containing a given integer is affected by its distance from the boundary. This creates substantial variation in the row sums, with∑i=1N(ℛi−ℛ¯)2=Ω​(N)\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\mathcal{R}_{i}-\bar{\mathcal{R}})^{2}=\Omega(N)and Theorem1.4therefore gives consistency of the pseudolikelihood estimator.

In the cyclic model, translation invariance makes all row sums equal. Moreover, the tensor satisfies the mean-field condition (4) and the spectral-gap condition (10); see LemmasB.5andB.6. Theorem1.13then implies thatTN​(X)=op​(1)T_{N}(X)=o_{p}(1)in the ferromagnetic regime. On the other hand, as in the edge-triangle ERGM, sufficiently strong antiferromagnetic interactions force near-maximizers of the variational problem to remain far from constant vectors. Establishing this fact for cyclic33-APs requires a more delicate argument, given in LemmaB.8. It follows that pseudolikelihood estimation is still consistent in the strongly antiferromagnetic regime.

## 2.3.Inhomogeneous Random Hypergraphs

Our final example comes from inhomogeneous random hypergraphs. In latent-kernel models,
edge probabilities are governed by latent vertex positions rather than by a single global
density; inhomogeneous random hypergraphs give the analogous framework for higher-order interactions, where
the probability of a hyperedge depends on the latent types of all participating vertices[bollobas2007phase,lovasz2012large,balasubramanian2021nonparametric,elek2012measure,zhao2015hypergraph].
Here we use such a kernel only as a source of structured random cubic tensors. Conditional
on the realized tensor, the statistical problem is to estimate the two Gibbs parameters(β,h)(\beta,h), not the kernelggitself.

Letg:[0,1]3→[0,1]g:[0,1]^{3}\to[0,1]be a symmetric continuous function. For each unordered triple{i,j,k}⊂[N]\{i,j,k\}\subset[N]with distinct entries, letξ{i,j,k}∼Ber​(g​(i/N,j/N,k/N))\xi_{\{i,j,k\}}\sim{\rm Ber}\bigl(g(i/N,j/N,k/N)\bigr)

independently over all such triples. DefineGN​(i,j,k)=ξ{i,j,k}G_{N}(i,j,k)=\xi_{\{i,j,k\}}wheneveri,j,ki,j,kare distinct, extend this definition symmetrically over permutations of the indices,
and setGN​(i,j,k)=0G_{N}(i,j,k)=0whenever{i,j,k}\{i,j,k\}are not all distinct. We then defineAi​j​k:=N−2​GN​(i,j,k).A_{ijk}:=N^{-2}G_{N}(i,j,k).Conditional onAA, writePβ,hAP^{A}_{\beta,h}for the Gibbs measure defined by (1).

The relevant deterministic profile isG​(x):=∫[0,1]2g​(x,y,z)​𝑑y​𝑑z,G¯:=∫[0,1]3g​(x,y,z)​𝑑x​𝑑y​𝑑z.G(x):=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}g(x,y,z)\,dy\,dz,\qquad\bar{G}:=\int_{[0,1]^{3}}g(x,y,z)\,dx\,dy\,dz.

This profile gives the limiting row sums of the tensor. IfGGis nonconstant, these row sums vary across coordinates, creating enough variation in the local fields for joint estimation. IfG≡G¯G\equiv\bar{G}andggis uniformly positive, the tensor is asymptotically homogeneous and well connected. In this case, pseudolikelihood estimation becomes ill-conditioned in the ferromagnetic regime with a nonnegative external field. The precise statement is as follows.

## Theorem 2.5.

LetℙA\mathbb{P}_{A}denote the law of the random tensorAA. The following conditional conclusions hold on an event withℙA\mathbb{P}_{A}-probability tending to one forX∼Pβ0,h0AX\sim P^{A}_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}.
- (a)

IfGGis not constant andΛ′​(h0)≠0\Lambda^{\prime}(h_{0})\neq 0, thenTN​(X)=ΩPβ0,h0A​(1).T_{N}(X)=\Omega_{P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}^{A}}(1).Consequently, the MPLE exists withPβ0,h0AP_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}^{A}probability tending to one and is jointlyN\sqrt{N}-consistent for(β0,h0)(\beta_{0},h_{0}).
- (b)

Assume thatG≡G¯G\equiv\bar{G}, thatinf[0,1]3g>0\inf_{[0,1]^{3}}g>0, and thatμ\muis stochastically nonnegative. Ifβ0>0\beta_{0}>0,h0⩾0h_{0}\geqslant 0, and eitherh0>0h_{0}>0orκ−⩾0\kappa_{-}\geqslant 0, thenTN​(X)=oPβ0,h0A​(1).T_{N}(X)=o_{P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}^{A}}(1).

## Remark 2.6.

Note that the conclusion of Theorem2.5(b) does not directly imply that estimation using pseudo-likelihood is impossible. However, extending the contiguity arguments of[GM20], one should be able to show that consistent estimation of both parameters is impossible in the above setting using any estimators, whenever the functionGGis constant. Since the arguments would be very similar, we skip it here.

## 3.Preliminaries

In this section, we first record and prove some necessary concentration results. Then we introduce two important lemmas which gives simpler criteria for mean-field and well-connectivity conditions. Finally, we record some auxiliary lemmas used in other proofs.

## 3.1.Concentration Results

We begin with the following definition of local perturbation of a configuration inℝN\mathbb{R}^{N}.

## Definition 3.1.

Fix a constanta∗:=κ−+κ+2a_{*}:=\frac{\kappa_{-}+\kappa_{+}}{2}, for a vectorx∈[κ−,κ+]Nx\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}, letx(i)∈[κ−,κ+]Nx^{(i)}\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}be the vector obtained by replacing theii-th coordinate ofxxbya∗a_{*}, i.e.xk(i)=xkx_{k}^{(i)}=x_{k}ifk≠ik\neq iandxk(k)=a∗.x_{k}^{(k)}=a_{*}.

## Lemma 3.2.

Let{gi}i=1N\{g_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}be a collection of functions defined on[κ−,κ+]N[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}. Assume it further satisfies the following properties,
- (i)

For everyi∈[N]i\in[N]there exists a finite constantdid_{i}, such that‖gi‖∞⩽di.\left\|g_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant d_{i}.
- (ii)

For eachi,j∈[N]i,j\in[N], there exists finite constantci​j⩾0c_{ij}\geqslant 0such thatmaxx∈[κ−,κ+]N⁡|gi​(x)−gi​(x(j))|⩽ci​j.\max_{x\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big|g_{i}(x)-g_{i}(x^{(j)})\big|\leqslant c_{ij}.

Then forX∼Pβ0,h0X\sim P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}, under the Assumption1.2, withbi​(X)=Λ′​(β0​mi​(X)+h0)b_{i}(X)=\Lambda^{\prime}(\beta_{0}m_{i}(X)+h_{0})we have𝔼⁡[(∑igi​(X)​(Xi−bi​(X)))2]⩽4​κ2​((1+γ​|β0|​κ3)​∑idi2+∑i,j(ci​i​dj+di​cj​i)),\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\big[\big(\sum_{i}g_{i}(X)(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))\big)^{2}\big]\leqslant 4\kappa^{2}\Big((1+\gamma|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{3})\sum_{i}d_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i,j}(c_{ii}d_{j}+d_{i}c_{ji})\Big),

## Proof.

We expand the square as𝔼⁡[(∑igi​(X)​(Xi−bi​(X)))2]\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\big[\big(\sum_{i}g_{i}(X)(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))\big)^{2}\big]=∑i,j𝔼[(gi(X)gj(X)(Xi−bi(X))(Xj−bj(X))].\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\Big[(g_{i}(X)g_{j}(X)(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))(X_{j}-b_{j}(X))\Big].(15)

Now consider the cases wheni=ji=jandi≠ji\neq jseparately.
- •

i=ji=j: In this case, using the fact|xi−bi​(x)|⩽2​κ\left|x_{i}-b_{i}(x)\right|\leqslant 2\kappafor eachi∈[N]i\in[N], the expectation in the RHS of (15) is bounded by4​κ2​di24\kappa^{2}d_{i}^{2}.
- •

i≠ji\neq j: In this case, the expectation in the RHS of (15) can be decomposed as follows:𝔼⁡[(gi​(X)−gi​(X(i)))​gj​(X)​(Xi−bi​(X))​(Xj−bj​(X))]+𝔼⁡[gi​(X(i))​(gj​(X)−gj​(X(i)))​(Xi−bi​(X))​(Xj−bj​(X))]+𝔼⁡[gi​(X(i))​gj​(X(i))​(Xi−bi​(X))​(Xj−bj​(X(i)))]+𝔼⁡[gi​(X(i))​gj​(X(i))​(Xi−bi​(X))​(bj​(X(i))−bj​(X))].\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\left[(g_{i}(X)-g_{i}(X^{(i)}))g_{j}(X)(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))(X_{j}-b_{j}(X))\right]\\
+&\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\left[g_{i}(X^{(i)})(g_{j}(X)-g_{j}(X^{(i)}))(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))(X_{j}-b_{j}(X))\right]\\
+&\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\left[g_{i}(X^{(i)})g_{j}(X^{(i)})(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))(X_{j}-b_{j}(X^{(i)}))\right]\\
+&\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\left[g_{i}(X^{(i)})g_{j}(X^{(i)})(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))(b_{j}(X^{(i)})-b_{j}(X))\right].\end{split}(16)

Since𝔼⁡[Xi∣X(i)]=bi​(X)\operatorname{\mathds{E}}[X_{i}\mid X^{(i)}]=b_{i}(X)andX(i)X^{(i)}does not depend onXiX_{i}, it follows that, fori≠ji\neq j,𝔼[gi(X(i))gj(X(i))(Xi−bi(X))(Xj−bj(X(i)))|X(i)]=0,\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\big[g_{i}(X^{(i)})g_{j}(X^{(i)})(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))(X_{j}-b_{j}(X^{(i)}))\big\rvert X^{(i)}\big]=0,

which implies that the third term in the RHS of (16) equals0. Using the bounds stated in the lemma, the sum of the first, second and fourth terms in the RHS of (16) can be bounded by4​κ2​(ci​i​dj+di​cj​i)+4​|β0|​κ5​di​dj​ℛj​i,\displaystyle 4\kappa^{2}\big(c_{ii}d_{j}+d_{i}c_{ji}\big)+4|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{5}d_{i}d_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji},

where, to bound the fourth term, we use the facts that|xi−bi​(x)|⩽2​κ|x_{i}-b_{i}(x)|\leqslant 2\kappaand|bj​(x)−bj​(x(i))|⩽|β0|​κ2​|mj​(x)−mj​(x(i))|⩽2​|β0|​κ4​∑kAj​i​k=2​|β0|​κ4​ℛj​i,\displaystyle|b_{j}(x)-b_{j}(x^{(i)})|\leqslant|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{2}|m_{j}(x)-m_{j}(x^{(i)})|\leqslant 2|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{4}\sum_{k}A_{jik}=2|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{4}\mathcal{R}_{ji},(17)

where the first inequality in (17) follows from the fact|Λ′​(a′)−Λ′​(a)|⩽supλΛ′′​(λ)⋅|a−a′|⩽κ2​|a−a′|.\displaystyle|\Lambda^{\prime}(a^{\prime})-\Lambda^{\prime}(a)|\leqslant\sup_{\lambda}\Lambda^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)\cdot|a-a^{\prime}|\leqslant\kappa^{2}|a-a^{\prime}|.(18)

The bound stated in the lemma follows on combining the above bounds, and using Assumption1.2to get∑i,jdi​dj​ℛj​i⩽‖ℛ‖op​‖𝐝‖22⩽γ​∑idi2.\sum_{i,j}d_{i}d_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji}\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|{\bf d}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\gamma\sum_{i}d_{i}^{2}.■\blacksquare

Applying the above lemma gives second-moment control for specific functions of the 3-tensor Ising model.

## Corollary 3.3.

LetX∼Pβ0,h0X\sim P_{\beta_{0},h_{0}}. whereC1,C2,C3,C4C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}andC5C_{5}are constants that only depend onβ0,γ,κ.\beta_{0},\gamma,\kappa.In particular, one can takeC1=4​κ2​(1+γ​|β0|​κ3+γ​κ2),C2=4​γ​κ4,C3=(8+4​γ​|β0|​κ3)​γ2​κ4,\displaystyle C_{1}=4\kappa^{2}\bigl(1+\gamma|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{3}+\gamma\kappa^{2}\bigr),\ \ \ C_{2}=4\gamma\kappa^{4},\ \ \ C_{3}=(8+4\gamma|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{3})\gamma^{2}\kappa^{4},C4=12​γ​κ4+48​γ2​κ6+12​γ3​κ8+108​|β0|​γ3​κ9,C5=72​|β0|​γ​κ9.\displaystyle C_{4}=12\gamma\kappa^{4}+48\gamma^{2}\kappa^{6}+12\gamma^{3}\kappa^{8}+108|\beta_{0}|\gamma^{3}\kappa^{9},\ \ \ C_{5}=72|\beta_{0}|\gamma\kappa^{9}.

If we further assume the mean-field condition (4), then
(22) can be bounded as𝔼⁡[(∑i,j,kAi​j​k​(Xi​Xj​Xk−bi​(X)​bj​(X)​bk​(X)))2]=o​(N2​(log⁡N)−1).\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\Big[\Big(\sum_{i,j,k}A_{ijk}(X_{i}X_{j}X_{k}-b_{i}(X)b_{j}(X)b_{k}(X))\Big)^{2}\Big]=o(N^{2}(\log N)^{-1}).(23)

## Proof.

Note that (20) is an immediate consequence of
(19) once we takeϕi≡ai.\phi_{i}\equiv a_{i}.The proofs of (19) and (21) follow from the application of Lemma3.2by settinggi​(x)=ϕi​(mi​(x))g_{i}(x)=\phi_{i}(m_{i}(x))and∑kℛi​k​xk\sum_{k}\mathcal{R}_{ik}x_{k}respectively. Fori,j∈[N]i,j\in[N],|gi​(x)−gi​(x(j))|⩽‖ϕi‖Lip​|mi​(x)−mi​(x(j))|⩽2​κ2​‖ϕi‖Lip​ℛi​j,|g_{i}(x)-g_{i}(x^{(j)})|\leqslant\|\phi_{i}\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}\,|m_{i}(x)-m_{i}(x^{(j)})|\leqslant 2\kappa^{2}\|\phi_{i}\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}\mathcal{R}_{ij},

so we may takeci​j:=2​κ2​‖ϕi‖Lip​ℛi​j.c_{ij}:=2\kappa^{2}\|\phi_{i}\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}\mathcal{R}_{ij}.Lemma3.2therefore gives𝔼​[(∑i(Xi−bi​(X))​ϕi​(mi​(X)))2]⩽4​κ2​(1+γ​|β0|​κ3)​∑i‖ϕi‖∞2+8​κ4​∑i,j‖ϕi‖∞​‖ϕj‖Lip​ℛj​i.\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{i}(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))\phi_{i}(m_{i}(X))\Big)^{2}\Big]\leqslant 4\kappa^{2}(1+\gamma|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{3})\sum_{i}\|\phi_{i}\|_{\infty}^{2}+8\kappa^{4}\sum_{i,j}\|\phi_{i}\|_{\infty}\|\phi_{j}\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}\mathcal{R}_{ji}.

Using2​u​v⩽u2+v22uv\leqslant u^{2}+v^{2}and the fact∑jℛi​j⩽γ\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ij}\leqslant\gammafor everyii, we have∑i,j‖ϕi‖∞​‖ϕj‖Lip​ℛj​i⩽γ2​∑i‖ϕi‖∞2+γ2​∑i‖ϕi‖Lip2.\sum_{i,j}\|\phi_{i}\|_{\infty}\|\phi_{j}\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}\mathcal{R}_{ji}\leqslant\frac{\gamma}{2}\sum_{i}\|\phi_{i}\|_{\infty}^{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}\sum_{i}\|\phi_{i}\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}.

Substituting this into the previous bound yields (19).

Ifgi​(x)=∑kℛi​k​xkg_{i}(x)=\sum_{k}\mathcal{R}_{ik}x_{k}, thendi=κ​γd_{i}=\kappa\gammaandci​j=κ​ℛi​jc_{ij}=\kappa\mathcal{R}_{ij}, which yields the bound (21).

Finally, we prove the bound (22). Using(u+v+w)2⩽3​(u2+v2+w2)(u+v+w)^{2}\leqslant 3(u^{2}+v^{2}+w^{2}), it suffices to bound the second moment of each term on the RHS. For the first term, note that∑j,kAi​j​k​xj​xk=mi​(x)\sum_{j,k}A_{ijk}x_{j}x_{k}=m_{i}(x), we can apply (19) withϕi​(t)=t\phi_{i}(t)=twith‖ϕi‖∞=γ​κ2\left\|\phi_{i}\right\|_{\infty}=\gamma\kappa^{2}and‖ϕi‖Lip=1\left\|\phi_{i}\right\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}=1to obtain𝔼⁡[(∑i,j,kAi​j​k​Xj​Xk​(Xi−bi​(X)))2]⩽4​N​γ2​κ6​(1+γ​|β0|​κ3+γ​κ2)+4​N​γ​κ4.\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\Big[\Big(\sum_{i,j,k}A_{ijk}X_{j}X_{k}(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))\Big)^{2}\Big]\leqslant 4N\gamma^{2}\kappa^{6}\bigl(1+\gamma|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{3}+\gamma\kappa^{2}\bigr)+4N\gamma\kappa^{4}.(24)

For the second term, we invoke Lemma3.2withgi​(x)=∑j,kAi​j​k​bj​(x)​xkg_{i}(x)=\sum_{j,k}A_{ijk}b_{j}(x)x_{k}. Observe that∥gi∥∞⩽κ2γ=:di\left\|g_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\kappa^{2}\gamma=:d_{i}and|gi​(x)−gi​(x(ℓ))|\displaystyle\big|g_{i}(x)-g_{i}(x^{(\ell)})\big|⩽∑jAi​j​ℓ​|bj​(x)​xℓ|+∑j,kAi​j​k​|bj​(x)−bj​(x(ℓ))|​|xk|\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j}A_{ij\ell}|b_{j}(x)x_{\ell}|+\sum_{j,k}A_{ijk}\big|b_{j}(x)-b_{j}(x^{(\ell)})\big||x_{k}|⩽(17)κ2ℛi​ℓ+2|β0|κ5∑jℛi​jℛj​ℓ=κ2ℛi​ℓ+2|β0|κ5(ℛ2)i​ℓ=:ci​ℓ.\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bound-tanh}}}{{\leqslant}}\kappa^{2}\mathcal{R}_{i\ell}+2|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{5}\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ij}\mathcal{R}_{j\ell}=\kappa^{2}\mathcal{R}_{i\ell}+2|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{5}(\mathcal{R}^{2})_{i\ell}=:c_{i\ell}.

Then we have𝔼⁡[(∑i,j,kAi​j​k​(Xi−bi​(X))​bj​(X)​Xk)2]\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\Big[\Big(\sum_{i,j,k}A_{ijk}(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))b_{j}(X)X_{k}\Big)^{2}\Big]⩽\displaystyle\leqslant4​κ2​((1+γ​|β0|​κ3)​N​γ2​κ4+∑i,j(2​|β0|​γ​κ7​(ℛ2)i​i+γ​κ4​ℛj​i+2​|β0|​γ​κ7​(ℛ2)j​i))\displaystyle 4\kappa^{2}\Bigg((1+\gamma|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{3})N\gamma^{2}\kappa^{4}+\sum_{i,j}\Big(2|\beta_{0}|\gamma\kappa^{7}(\mathcal{R}^{2})_{ii}+\gamma\kappa^{4}\mathcal{R}_{ji}+2|\beta_{0}|\gamma\kappa^{7}(\mathcal{R}^{2})_{ji}\Big)\Bigg)=\displaystyle=4​κ2​((1+γ​|β0|​κ3)​N​γ2​κ4+2​|β0|​γ​κ7​N​Tr​(ℛ2)+γ​κ4​𝟏T​ℛ​𝟏+2​|β0|​γ​κ7​𝟏T​ℛ2​𝟏).\displaystyle 4\kappa^{2}\Big((1+\gamma|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{3})N\gamma^{2}\kappa^{4}+2|\beta_{0}|\gamma\kappa^{7}N\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})+\gamma\kappa^{4}\mathbf{1}^{T}\mathcal{R}\mathbf{1}+2|\beta_{0}|\gamma\kappa^{7}\mathbf{1}^{T}\mathcal{R}^{2}\mathbf{1}\Big).(25)

Similarly, we can bound the second moment of the third term. In the setting of Lemma3.2, takegi​(x)=∑j,kAi​j​k​bj​(x)​bk​(x)g_{i}(x)=\sum_{j,k}A_{ijk}b_{j}(x)b_{k}(x). Note that∥gi∥∞⩽κ2γ=:di\left\|g_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\kappa^{2}\gamma=:d_{i}and|gi​(x)−gi​(x(ℓ))|\displaystyle\bigl|g_{i}(x)-g_{i}(x^{(\ell)})\bigr|⩽∑j,kAi​j​k​|bj​(x)−bj​(x(ℓ))|​|xk|+∑jAi​j​ℓ​|bj​(x(ℓ))|​|xℓ−a∗|\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j,k}A_{ijk}\bigl|b_{j}(x)-b_{j}(x^{(\ell)})\bigr|\,|x_{k}|+\sum_{j}A_{ij\ell}|b_{j}(x^{(\ell)})|\,|x_{\ell}-a_{*}|⩽κ2ℛi​ℓ+2|β0|κ5∑jℛi​jℛj​ℓ=κ2ℛi​ℓ+2|β0|κ5(ℛ2)i​ℓ=:ci​ℓ.\displaystyle\leqslant\kappa^{2}\mathcal{R}_{i\ell}+2|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{5}\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ij}\mathcal{R}_{j\ell}=\kappa^{2}\mathcal{R}_{i\ell}+2|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{5}(\mathcal{R}^{2})_{i\ell}=:c_{i\ell}.

Thus we have𝔼⁡[(∑i,j,kAi​j​k​(Xi−bi​(X))​bj​(X)​bk​(X))2]\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\Big[\Big(\sum_{i,j,k}A_{ijk}(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))b_{j}(X)b_{k}(X)\Big)^{2}\Big]⩽\displaystyle\leqslant4​κ2​((1+γ​|β0|​κ3)​N​γ2​κ4+4​|β0|​γ​κ7​∑i,j((ℛ2)i​i+(ℛ2)j​i))\displaystyle 4\kappa^{2}\Big((1+\gamma|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{3})N\gamma^{2}\kappa^{4}+4|\beta_{0}|\gamma\kappa^{7}\sum_{i,j}\big((\mathcal{R}^{2})_{ii}+(\mathcal{R}^{2})_{ji}\big)\Big)=\displaystyle=4​κ2​((1+γ​|β0|​κ3)​N​γ2​κ4+4​|β0|​γ​κ7​N​Tr​(ℛ2)+4​|β0|​γ​κ7​𝟏T​ℛ2​𝟏).\displaystyle 4\kappa^{2}\Big((1+\gamma|\beta_{0}|\kappa^{3})N\gamma^{2}\kappa^{4}+4|\beta_{0}|\gamma\kappa^{7}N\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})+4|\beta_{0}|\gamma\kappa^{7}\boldsymbol{1}^{T}\mathcal{R}^{2}\boldsymbol{1}\Big).(26)

Combining (24), (25) and (26), the bound (22) now follows from the fact that𝟏T​ℛ​𝟏⩽N​‖ℛ‖op⩽γ​N\mathbf{1}^{T}\mathcal{R}\mathbf{1}\leqslant N\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\gamma Nand𝟏T​ℛ2​𝟏⩽N​‖ℛ‖op2⩽γ2​N.\mathbf{1}^{T}\mathcal{R}^{2}\mathbf{1}\leqslant N\|\mathcal{R}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\gamma^{2}N.

To obtain (23), note that the mean-field condition (4) and the inequality in Lemma3.4yieldsTr​(ℛ2)=o​(N​(log⁡N)−1)\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})=o(N(\log N)^{-1}).■\blacksquare

## 3.2.Mean-Field and Connectivity Criteria

Recall thatℛ\mathcal{R}is the symmetricN×NN\times Nmatrix with entriesℛi​j=∑kAi​j​k\mathcal{R}_{ij}=\sum_{k}A_{ijk}, and thatAi=(Ai​j​k)j,k∈[N]A_{i}=(A_{ijk})_{j,k\in[N]}denotes theii-th matrix slice of the tensor. The next lemma relates the mean-field quantity‖∑iAi2‖op\big\|\sum_{i}A_{i}^{2}\big\|_{\rm op}to the squared Frobenius norm ofℛ\mathcal{R}. Part (a) gives the general lower boundN​‖∑iAi2‖op⩾Tr​(ℛ2)N\big\|\sum_{i}A_{i}^{2}\big\|_{\rm op}\geqslant\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2}). Although the reverse inequality does not hold in general, part (b) shows that the two quantities are comparable when the nonzero entries ofℛ\mathcal{R}are comparable. We call this the strong pseudo-regularity condition in (6).

## Lemma 3.4.

LetAAbe a symmetric33-tensor with non-negative entries.
- (a)

We haveTr​(ℛ2)⩽N​‖∑iAi2‖op⩽N​‖ℛ‖op2.\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})\leqslant N\big\|\sum_{i}A^{2}_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant N\|\mathcal{R}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{op}}.
- (b)

Assume further thatAAsatisfies the row-boundedness condition in Assumption1.2and the non-degeneracy condition (7). Suppose there exist constantK>0K>0such that the following strong pseudo-regularity condition (6) holds.
Then we haveN​‖∑iAi2‖op⩽γ​Kα​Tr​(ℛ2)N\Big\|\sum_{i}A_{i}^{2}\Big\|_{\rm op}\leqslant\frac{\gamma K}{\alpha}{\rm{Tr}}(\mathcal{R}^{2})

whereα\alphais the lower bound ofℛ¯\bar{\mathcal{R}}in (7) andγ\gammais the upper bound of row sum ofAAin the Assumption1.2. In particular, ifTr​(ℛ2)=o​(N/log⁡N){\rm{Tr}}(\mathcal{R}^{2})=o(N/\log N), then the mean-field condition (4) holds.

## Proof.

(a) We begin by proving the first inequality. Using the identityAi​𝟏=ℛ​𝐞𝐢A_{i}\mathbf{1}=\mathcal{R}\mathbf{e_{i}}, we have𝟏T​(∑iAi2)​𝟏\displaystyle\mathbf{1}^{T}\big(\sum_{i}A_{i}^{2}\big)\mathbf{1}=∑i𝐞𝐢T​ℛ2​𝐞𝐢=Tr​(ℛ2).\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\mathbf{e_{i}}^{T}\mathcal{R}^{2}\mathbf{e_{i}}=\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2}).

The inequality now follows from the fact that𝟏T​(∑iAi2)​𝟏⩽N​‖∑iAi2‖op\mathbf{1}^{T}\big(\sum_{i}A_{i}^{2}\big)\mathbf{1}\leqslant N\big\|\sum_{i}A^{2}_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

For the second inequality, we note that since the entries ofℛ\mathcal{R}are non-negative,‖ℛ2‖op=supx∈ℝ+N:‖x‖2=1∑i,jxi​xj​(ℛ2)i,j.\|\mathcal{R}^{2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{N}:\|x\|_{2}=1}\sum_{i,j}x_{i}x_{j}(\mathcal{R}^{2})_{i,j}.

Using the decompositionℛ=∑kAk\mathcal{R}=\sum_{k}A_{k}, we then bound‖ℛ2‖op\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}^{2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=supx∈ℝ+N:‖x‖2=1∑i,jxi​xj​(∑k(Ak2)i,j+∑k≠k′(Ak​Ak′)i,j)\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{N}:\|x\|_{2}=1}\sum_{i,j}x_{i}x_{j}\big(\sum_{k}(A_{k}^{2})_{i,j}+\sum_{k\neq k^{\prime}}(A_{k}A_{k^{\prime}})_{i,j}\big)⩾supx∈ℝ+N:‖x‖2=1∑i,jxi​xj​∑k(Ak2)i,j=‖∑kAk2‖op.\displaystyle\geqslant\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{N}:\|x\|_{2}=1}\sum_{i,j}x_{i}x_{j}\sum_{k}(A_{k}^{2})_{i,j}=\big\|\sum_{k}A^{2}_{k}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

(b) SetM:=∑iAi2.M:=\sum_{i}A_{i}^{2}.SinceMMhas nonnegative entries,‖M‖op⩽maxj​∑kMj​k.\|M\|_{\rm op}\leqslant\max_{j}\sum_{k}M_{jk}.

For eachjj,∑kMj​k=∑i,ℓ,kAi​j​ℓ​Ai​ℓ​k=∑i,ℓAj​i​ℓ​ℛi​ℓ⩽(maxa,b⁡ℛa​b)​ℛj⩽γ​maxa,b⁡ℛa​b.\sum_{k}M_{jk}=\sum_{i,\ell,k}A_{ij\ell}A_{i\ell k}=\sum_{i,\ell}A_{ji\ell}\mathcal{R}_{i\ell}\leqslant\left(\max_{a,b}\mathcal{R}_{ab}\right)\mathcal{R}_{j}\leqslant\gamma\max_{a,b}\mathcal{R}_{ab}.

Letρ:=minℛa​b>0⁡ℛa​b.\rho:=\min_{\mathcal{R}_{ab}>0}\mathcal{R}_{ab}.By the strong pseudo-regularity assumption (6),maxa,b⁡ℛa​b⩽K​ρ\max_{a,b}\mathcal{R}_{ab}\leqslant K\rho.
On the other hand,Tr​(ℛ2)=∑a,bℛa​b2⩾ρ​∑a,bℛa​b=ρ​∑aℛa⩾α​N​ρ.\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})=\sum_{a,b}\mathcal{R}_{ab}^{2}\geqslant\rho\sum_{a,b}\mathcal{R}_{ab}=\rho\sum_{a}\mathcal{R}_{a}\geqslant\alpha N\rho.

Thereforemaxa,b⁡ℛa​b⩽K​ρ⩽Kα​Tr​(ℛ2)N.\max_{a,b}\mathcal{R}_{ab}\leqslant K\rho\leqslant\frac{K}{\alpha}\frac{\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})}{N}.Combining the estimates gives‖∑iAi2‖op=‖M‖op⩽γ​Kα​Tr​(ℛ2)N,\Big\|\sum_{i}A_{i}^{2}\Big\|_{\rm op}=\|M\|_{\rm op}\leqslant\frac{\gamma K}{\alpha}\frac{\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})}{N},

which proves part (b).■\blacksquare

Under the same the strong pseudo-regularity assumption (6),
the next lemma gives an easy-to-check sufficient condition, namely anunweightedspectral gap for the support graph ofℛ\mathcal{R}, for theweightedspectral gap condition (10).

## Lemma 3.5.

Assume thatAAsatisfies the hypotheses of Lemma3.4(b). LetBi​j:=𝟏{ℛi​j>0}B_{ij}:=\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{R}_{ij}>0\}}denote the adjacency matrix of the support graph ofℛ\mathcal{R}on vertex set[N][N].
LetDB:=diag​(d1,B,…,dN,B)D_{B}:={\rm diag}(d_{1,B},\ldots,d_{N,B}), wheredi,B:=∑jBi​jd_{i,B}:=\sum_{j}B_{ij}is the degree of vertexi∈[N]i\in[N]. If this support graph has a spectral gap, that is,1−λ2​(DB−1​B)⩾δ1-\lambda_{2}(D_{B}^{-1}B)\geqslant\deltafor some constantδ>0\delta>0, thenℛ\mathcal{R}satisfies the spectral gap condition (10). More precisely, withD=diag​(ℛ1,…,ℛN)D={\rm diag}(\mathcal{R}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{R}_{N}),1−λ2​(D−1​ℛ)⩾δK.1-\lambda_{2}(D^{-1}\mathcal{R})\geqslant\frac{\delta}{K}.

Moreover, if all off-diagonal entries ofℛ\mathcal{R}are positive and comparable in the sense thatmaxi≠j⁡ℛi​j⩽K​mini≠j⁡ℛi​j,\displaystyle\max_{i\neq j}\mathcal{R}_{ij}\leqslant K\min_{i\neq j}\mathcal{R}_{ij},(27)

then1−λ2​(D−1​ℛ)⩾K−1.1-\lambda_{2}(D^{-1}\mathcal{R})\geqslant K^{-1}.

## Proof.

By the variational characterization of the spectral gap,1−λ2​(D−1​ℛ)=infu≢const12​∑i,jℛi​j​(ui−uj)2mina∈ℝ​∑iℛi​(ui−a)2.1-\lambda_{2}(D^{-1}\mathcal{R})=\inf_{u\not\equiv{\rm const}}\frac{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}\mathcal{R}_{ij}(u_{i}-u_{j})^{2}}{\min_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(u_{i}-a)^{2}}.

It follows from (6) thatρ​Bi​j⩽ℛi​j⩽K​ρ​Bi​j\rho B_{ij}\leqslant\mathcal{R}_{ij}\leqslant K\rho B_{ij}whereρ:=minℛa​b>0⁡ℛa​b.\rho:=\min_{\mathcal{R}_{ab}>0}\mathcal{R}_{ab}.Hence,
we haveℛi⩽K​ρ​di,B\mathcal{R}_{i}\leqslant K\rho d_{i,B}for everyi∈[N]i\in[N], and1−λ2​(D−1​ℛ)⩾1K​infu≢const12​∑i,jBi​j​(ui−uj)2mina∈ℝ​∑idi,B​(ui−a)2⩾δK.1-\lambda_{2}(D^{-1}\mathcal{R})\geqslant\frac{1}{K}\inf_{u\not\equiv{\rm const}}\frac{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}B_{ij}(u_{i}-u_{j})^{2}}{\min_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{i}d_{i,B}(u_{i}-a)^{2}}\geqslant\frac{\delta}{K}.

This proves the first assertion.

Finally, if (27) holds,
then the support graphBBis the complete graph and the strong pseudo-regularity condition holds. The complete graph has spectral gap1+1N−1⩾1=δ1+\frac{1}{N-1}\geqslant 1=\delta, so the previous bound gives1−λ2​(D−1​ℛ)⩾K−1,1-\lambda_{2}(D^{-1}\mathcal{R})\geqslant K^{-1},which completes the proof of the lemma.■\blacksquare

By continuity and compactness, there existη′>0\eta^{\prime}>0andc>0c>0such that|F​(t,r)|⩾c|F(t,r)|\geqslant cwhenever|t|⩽η′|t|\leqslant\eta^{\prime}andr∈[0,γ]r\in[0,\gamma]. Hencelim supNℙ​(X∈𝒜N,|b¯​(X)|⩽η′)⩽lim supNℙ​(X∈𝒜N∩𝒢Nc)=0.\limsup_{N}\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{N},|\bar{b}(X)|\leqslant\eta^{\prime})\leqslant\limsup_{N}\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{N}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{c})=0.

SetM:=|β|​γ​κ2+|h|M:=|\beta|\gamma\kappa^{2}+|h|. By Assumption1.2,|mi​(x)|⩽γ​κ2|m_{i}(x)|\leqslant\gamma\kappa^{2}for alli∈[N]i\in[N], and henceβ​mi​(x)+h∈[−M,M]\beta m_{i}(x)+h\in[-M,M]. SinceΛ′\Lambda^{\prime}is continuous and takes values in(κ−,κ+)(\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}), there existsη′′>0\eta^{\prime\prime}>0such thatΛ′​([−M,M])⊂[κ−+η′′,κ+−η′′]\Lambda^{\prime}([-M,M])\subset[\kappa_{-}+\eta^{\prime\prime},\kappa_{+}-\eta^{\prime\prime}]. Therefore,b¯​(x)∈[κ−+η′′,κ+−η′′]\bar{b}(x)\in[\kappa_{-}+\eta^{\prime\prime},\kappa_{+}-\eta^{\prime\prime}]for everyx∈[κ−,κ+]Nx\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}. Takingη=min⁡{η′,η′′}>0\eta=\min\{\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}\}>0givesℙ​(X∈𝒜N,b¯​(X)∈Jηc)→0.\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{N},\ \bar{b}(X)\in J_{\eta}^{c})\to 0.

## Definition 4.2.

Letμ,Λ,Φ,κ−,κ+\mu,\Lambda,\Phi,\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}be as in Definition1.1.

Equivalently, underQtQ_{t}, the coordinatesX1,…,XNX_{1},\dots,X_{N}are i.i.d. with common lawμΦ​(t)\mu_{\Phi(t)}, and hence𝔼Qt​[Xi]=t\mathbb{E}_{Q_{t}}[X_{i}]=tandVarQt​(Xi)=v​(t),\mathrm{Var}_{Q_{t}}(X_{i})=v(t),wherev​(t)=Λ′′​(Φ​(t))v(t)=\Lambda^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(t))fort∈(κ−,κ+)t\in(\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+})andv​(κ±)=0.v(\kappa_{\pm})=0.

## Lemma 4.3.

Under the Assumption1.2, forX∼Pβ,hX\sim P_{\beta,h}. Suppose there existsεN→0\varepsilon_{N}\to 0and a sequence of low complexity sets(EN)N⩾1(E_{N})_{N\geqslant 1}in the sense of Definition1.8such thatlim infNPβ,h​(|f​(X)−f​(b​(X))|⩽N​εN​and​b​(X)∈EN)>0.\displaystyle\liminf_{N}P_{\beta,h}(\left|f(X)-f(b(X))\right|\leqslant N\varepsilon_{N}\ \text{and}\ b(X)\in E_{N})>0.(46)

Then the following holds.
- (i)

We havelog⁡ZN​(β,h)=supy∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(y)−I​(y))+o​(N).\displaystyle\log Z_{N}(\beta,h)=\sup_{y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)+o(N).
- (ii)

Suppose that there exists a sequence of sets(CN)N⩾1(C_{N})_{N\geqslant 1}withCN⊆[κ−,κ+]NC_{N}\subseteq[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}such thatlim infN1N​(supy∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(y)−I​(y))−supy∈CN(f​(y)−I​(y)))>0.\liminf_{N}\frac{1}{N}\Big(\sup_{y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)-\sup_{y\in C_{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)\Big)>0.

Then we havelimNPβ,h​(|f​(X)−f​(b​(X))|⩽N​εN,b​(X)∈CN∩EN)=0.\lim_{N}P_{\beta,h}(|f(X)-f(b(X))|\leqslant N\varepsilon_{N},b(X)\in C_{N}\cap E_{N})=0.
- (iii)

Assume thatΛ′​(h)≠0\Lambda^{\prime}(h)\neq 0, and there existsη>0\eta>0such thatlim supN→∞supt∈Jη1N​log⁡Qt​(𝒜N)<0,\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{t\in J_{\eta}}\frac{1}{N}\log Q_{t}(\mathcal{A}_{N})<0,

whereQtQ_{t}as in Definition4.2,JηJ_{\eta}and𝒜N\mathcal{A}_{N}are as defined in Lemma4.1. ThenlimN→∞Pβ,h​(𝒜N)=0.\lim_{N\to\infty}P_{\beta,h}(\mathcal{A}_{N})=0.

## Proof.

We first prove a general bound, that we will use to verify both parts (i) and (ii).
By Lemma4.1part (i), there exists a sequence of non-negative reals(εN′)N⩾1(\varepsilon_{N}^{\prime})_{N\geqslant 1}converging to0, such thatPβ,h​(|g​(X,b​(X))−I​(b​(X))|⩽N​εN′)→1.\displaystyle P_{\beta,h}(|g(X,b(X))-I(b(X))|\leqslant N\varepsilon_{N}^{\prime})\to 1.(47)

LetDN⊆[κ−,κ+]ND_{N}\subseteq[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}be arbitrary, and setℬN=ℬN(DN):={x∈[κ−,κ+]N:|f​(x)−f​(b​(x))|⩽N​εN,b(x)∈DN∩EN,|g(x,b(x))−I(b(x))|⩽NεN′}.\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{N}=\mathcal{B}_{N}(D_{N}):=\big\{x\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}:&|f(x)-f(b(x))|\leqslant N\varepsilon_{N},\\
&b(x)\in D_{N}\cap E_{N},\ |g(x,b(x))-I(b(x))|\leqslant N\varepsilon_{N}^{\prime}\big\}.\end{split}(48)

Then we have∫x∈ℬNexp⁡(f​(x))​𝑑μ⊗N\displaystyle\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}_{N}}\exp(f(x))d\mu^{\otimes N}⩽exp⁡(N​(εN+εN′))​∫x∈ℬNexp⁡(f​(b​(x))+g​(x,b​(x))−I​(b​(x)))​𝑑μ⊗N\displaystyle\leqslant\exp(N(\varepsilon_{N}+\varepsilon_{N}^{\prime}))\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}_{N}}\exp\big(f(b(x))+g(x,b(x))-I(b(x))\big)d\mu^{\otimes N}⩽exp⁡(o​(N)+supy∈DN(f​(y)−I​(y)))​∫x∈ℬNexp⁡(g​(x,b​(x)))​𝑑μ⊗N.\displaystyle\leqslant\exp\Big(o(N)+\sup_{y\in D_{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)\Big)\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}_{N}}\exp(g(x,b(x)))d\mu^{\otimes N}.(49)

Recall that from the proof of Lemma4.1(iii),bi​(x)=Λ′​(β​mi​(x)+h)∈[κ−+η′′,κ+−η′′]for some​η′′>0.b_{i}(x)=\Lambda^{\prime}(\beta m_{i}(x)+h)\in[\kappa_{-}+\eta^{\prime\prime},\kappa_{+}-\eta^{\prime\prime}]\quad\text{for some }\eta^{\prime\prime}>0.

Proceeding to estimate the last term in the RHS of (4), note that ifx∈ℬNx\in\mathcal{B}_{N}thenb​(x)∈EN∩[κ−+η′′,κ+−η′′]Nb(x)\in E_{N}\cap[\kappa_{-}+\eta^{\prime\prime},\kappa_{+}-\eta^{\prime\prime}]^{N},ENE_{N}is a low complexity set. Thus, for everyε>0\varepsilon>0, there exists a set𝒟N​(ε)\mathcal{D}_{N}(\varepsilon), obtained if necessary by projecting each point of a low-complexity net forENE_{N}coordinatewise onto the interval[κ−+η′′,κ+−η′′][\kappa_{-}+\eta^{\prime\prime},\kappa_{+}-\eta^{\prime\prime}], such that𝒟N​(ε)\mathcal{D}_{N}(\varepsilon)is anε​N\varepsilon\sqrt{N}-net ofEN∩[κ−+η′′,κ+−η′′]NE_{N}\cap[\kappa_{-}+\eta^{\prime\prime},\kappa_{+}-\eta^{\prime\prime}]^{N}in Euclidean metric andlog⁡|𝒟N​(ε)|=o​(N)\log|\mathcal{D}_{N}(\varepsilon)|=o(N). Consequently, for everyx∈[κ−,κ+]Nx\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}such thatb​(x)∈ENb(x)\in E_{N}, there existsy∈𝒟N​(ε)y\in\mathcal{D}_{N}(\varepsilon)such that‖b​(x)−y‖22⩽N​ε2\|b(x)-y\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N\varepsilon^{2}. Note that for functiong​(u,t)=u​Φ​(t)−Λ​(Φ​(t))g(u,t)=u\Phi(t)-\Lambda(\Phi(t)), whereu∈[κ−,κ+],t∈[κ−+η′′,κ+−η′′]u\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}],t\in[\kappa_{-}+\eta^{\prime\prime},\kappa_{+}-\eta^{\prime\prime}], we have|∂tg​(u,t)|=|(u−t)​Φ′​(t)|⩽C.\left|\partial_{t}g(u,t)\right|=\left|(u-t)\Phi^{\prime}(t)\right|\leqslant C.

By the mean value theorem, and sincebi​(x),yi∈[κ−+η′′,κ+−η′′]b_{i}(x),y_{i}\in[\kappa_{-}+\eta^{\prime\prime},\kappa_{+}-\eta^{\prime\prime}], it follows that|g​(x,b​(x))−g​(x,y)|\displaystyle|g(x,b(x))-g(x,y)|=|∑ig​(xi,bi​(x))−g​(xi,yi)|\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{i}g(x_{i},b_{i}(x))-g(x_{i},y_{i})\Big|⩽C​∑i|bi​(x)−yi|⩽C​N​∑i(bi−yi)2⩽C​N​ε.\displaystyle\leqslant C\sum_{i}\big|b_{i}(x)-y_{i}\big|\leqslant C\sqrt{N}\sqrt{\sum_{i}(b_{i}-y_{i})^{2}}\leqslant CN\varepsilon.

This gives∫x∈ℬNeg​(x,b​(x))​𝑑μ⊗N⩽\displaystyle\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}_{N}}e^{g(x,b(x))}d\mu^{\otimes N}\leqslant∑y∈𝒟N​(ε)∫x:‖b​(x)−y‖22⩽N​ε2eg​(x,b​(x))​𝑑μ⊗N\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\mathcal{D}_{N}(\varepsilon)}\int_{x:\|b(x)-y\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N\varepsilon^{2}}e^{g(x,b(x))}d\mu^{\otimes N}⩽\displaystyle\leqslanteC​N​ε​∑y∈𝒟N​(ε)∫x:‖b​(x)−y‖22⩽N​ε2eg​(x,y)​𝑑μ⊗N\displaystyle e^{CN\varepsilon}\sum_{y\in\mathcal{D}_{N}(\varepsilon)}\int_{x:\|b(x)-y\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N\varepsilon^{2}}e^{g(x,y)}d\mu^{\otimes N}⩽\displaystyle\leqslanteC​N​ε​∑y∈𝒟N​(ε)∫x∈[κ−,κ+]Neg​(x,y)​𝑑μ⊗N=eC​N​ε​|𝒟N​(ε)|,\displaystyle e^{CN\varepsilon}\sum_{y\in\mathcal{D}_{N}(\varepsilon)}\int_{x\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}e^{g(x,y)}d\mu^{\otimes N}=e^{CN\varepsilon}|\mathcal{D}_{N}(\varepsilon)|,(50)

where the last line uses the fact that for anyy∈[κ−+η′′,κ+−η′′]Ny\in[\kappa_{-}+\eta^{\prime\prime},\kappa_{+}-\eta^{\prime\prime}]^{N}we have∫x∈[κ−,κ+]Neg​(x,y)​𝑑μ⊗N=1.\displaystyle\int_{x\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}e^{g(x,y)}d\mu^{\otimes N}=1.

Sincelog⁡|𝒟N​(ε)|=o​(N)\log|\mathcal{D}_{N}(\varepsilon)|=o(N), combining (4) and (4) we getlim supN1N​[log​∫x∈ℬNexp⁡(f​(x))​𝑑μ⊗N−supy∈DN(f​(y)−I​(y))]⩽ε.\displaystyle\limsup_{N}\frac{1}{N}\Big[\log\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}_{N}}\exp(f(x))d\mu^{\otimes N}-\sup_{y\in D_{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)\Big]\leqslant\varepsilon.

Sinceε>0\varepsilon>0is arbitrary, this giveslim supN1N​[log​∫x∈ℬNexp⁡(f​(x))​𝑑μ⊗N−supy∈DN(f​(y)−I​(y))]⩽0.\displaystyle\limsup_{N}\frac{1}{N}\Big[\log\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}_{N}}\exp(f(x))d\mu^{\otimes N}-\sup_{y\in D_{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)\Big]\leqslant 0.(51)

Armed with this estimate, we now start with the proof of part (i).

(i)
LetℬN\mathcal{B}_{N}be as defined in (48) withDN=[κ−,κ+]ND_{N}=[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}. Then using (46) and (47), it follows thatδ:=lim infNℙ​(ℬN)>0.\delta:=\liminf_{N}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{N})>0.Thus, for allNNlarge enough we haveℙ​(ℬN)⩾δ/2\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{N})\geqslant\delta/2, which givesZN​(β,h)=∫[κ−,κ+]Nef​(x)​𝑑μ⊗N⩽2δ​∫x∈ℬNef​(x)​𝑑μ⊗N.\displaystyle Z_{N}(\beta,h)=\int_{[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}e^{f(x)}d\mu^{\otimes N}\leqslant\frac{2}{\delta}\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}_{N}}e^{f(x)}d\mu^{\otimes N}.

Sinceδ>0\delta>0is fixed, takinglog\log, dividing byNN, and lettingN→∞N\to\inftyon both sides of the above equation, we getlim supN1N​[log⁡ZN​(β,h)−supy∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(y)−I​(y))]⩽0,\displaystyle\limsup_{N}\frac{1}{N}\Big[\log Z_{N}(\beta,h)-\sup_{y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)\Big]\leqslant 0,

where we use (51) withDN=[κ−,κ+]ND_{N}=[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}. This gives the desired upper bound for the asymptotics oflog⁡ZN​(β,h).\log Z_{N}(\beta,h).The lower bound follows by invoking[Yan20, Theorem 1], which giveslog⁡ZN​(β,h)⩾supy∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(y)−I​(y)).\log Z_{N}(\beta,h)\geqslant\sup_{y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big).

This completes the proof of part (i).

(ii)
LetℬN\mathcal{B}_{N}be as defined in (48) withDN=CND_{N}=C_{N}, whereCNC_{N}is in the statement of the lemma. By (47), it suffices to show thatℙ​(ℬN)→0\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{N})\to 0. To this effect, note thatℙ​(ℬN)=1ZN​(β,h)​∫x∈ℬNef​(x)​𝑑μ⊗N.\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{N})=\frac{1}{Z_{N}(\beta,h)}\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}_{N}}e^{f(x)}d\mu^{\otimes N}.

Invoking (51) withDN=CND_{N}=C_{N}, we obtainlog⁡(∫x∈ℬNef​(x)​𝑑μ⊗N)⩽supy∈CN(f​(y)−I​(y))+o​(N).\displaystyle\log\Big(\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}_{N}}e^{f(x)}d\mu^{\otimes N}\Big)\leqslant\sup_{y\in C_{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)+o(N).

On the other hand, by part (i) above, we getlog⁡ZN​(β,h)=supy∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(y)−I​(y))+o​(N).\displaystyle\log Z_{N}(\beta,h)=\sup_{y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)+o(N).

Therefore,1N​log⁡ℙ​(ℬN)⩽1N​[supy∈CN(f​(y)−I​(y))−supy∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(y)−I​(y))]+o​(1).\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{N})\leqslant\frac{1}{N}\Big[\sup_{y\in C_{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)-\sup_{y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)\Big]+o(1).

By hypothesis of part (ii), we deduce thatlim supNN−1​log⁡ℙ​(ℬN)<0.\limsup_{N}N^{-1}\log\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{N})<0.This completes the proof of part(ii).

(iii) Assume, for contradiction, thatlim supN→∞Pβ,h​(X∈𝒜N)>0.\limsup_{N\to\infty}P_{\beta,h}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{N})>0.Chooseη>0\eta>0such that Lemma4.1part (iii) holds withJ2​ηJ_{2\eta}. Let𝒜N(1)\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(1)}be as in (28). By (29),ℙ​(X∉𝒜N(1))→0.\mathbb{P}\big(X\not\in\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(1)}\big)\to 0.Also, by Lemma4.1part (ii), there exists a sequenceδN↓0\delta_{N}\downarrow 0such thatPβ,h​(X∈𝒜N,|f​(X)−f​(X¯​𝟏)|>N​δN)→0.P_{\beta,h}\left(X\in\mathcal{A}_{N},\left|f(X)-f(\bar{X}\mathbf{1})\right|>N\delta_{N}\right)\to 0.

Finally, by Lemma4.1part (iii),Pβ,h​(X∈𝒜N,b¯​(X)∉J2​η)→0.P_{\beta,h}\left(X\in\mathcal{A}_{N},\bar{b}(X)\notin J_{2\eta}\right)\to 0.DefineℬN:=𝒜N∩𝒜N(1)∩{|f​(x)−f​(x¯​𝟏)|⩽N​δN}∩{b¯​(x)∈J2​η}.\mathcal{B}_{N}:=\mathcal{A}_{N}\cap\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(1)}\cap\left\{\left|f(x)-f(\bar{x}\mathbf{1})\right|\leqslant N\delta_{N}\right\}\cap\{\bar{b}(x)\in J_{2\eta}\}.

Then, we conclude thatlim supN→∞ℙ​(X∈ℬN)>0.\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{B}_{N})>0.

We now estimate the probability ofℬN\mathcal{B}_{N}from above. Ifx∈ℬNx\in\mathcal{B}_{N}, thenx∈𝒜N(1)x\in\mathcal{A}_{N}^{(1)}gives|x¯−b¯​(x)|⩽εN′\left|\bar{x}-\bar{b}(x)\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{N}^{\prime}.
Sinceb¯​(x)∈J2​η\bar{b}(x)\in J_{2\eta}, it follows that, for all sufficiently largeNN,x¯∈Jη.\bar{x}\in J_{\eta}.Moreover, onℬN\mathcal{B}_{N},f​(x)⩽f​(x¯​𝟏)+N​δN=β3​x¯3​∑i,j,kAi​j​k+h​N​x¯+N​δN=N​βN​x¯3+N​h​x¯+N​δN,\displaystyle f(x)\leqslant f(\bar{x}\mathbf{1})+N\delta_{N}=\frac{\beta}{3}\bar{x}^{3}\sum_{i,j,k}A_{ijk}+hN\bar{x}+N\delta_{N}=N\beta_{N}\bar{x}^{3}+Nh\bar{x}+N\delta_{N},

whereβN:=β​ℛ¯/3\beta_{N}:=\beta\bar{\mathcal{R}}/3. Therefore∫ℬNef​(x)​𝑑μ⊗N​(x)⩽eN​δN​∫ℬNexp⁡{N​βN​x¯3+N​h​x¯}​𝑑μ⊗N​(x)\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{N}}e^{f(x)}d\mu^{\otimes N}(x)\leqslant e^{N\delta_{N}}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{N}}\exp\{N\beta_{N}\bar{x}^{3}+Nh\bar{x}\}d\mu^{\otimes N}(x)=\displaystyle=eN​δN∫ℬNexp(NβNx¯3+Nhx¯−NI(x¯)+Φ(x¯)Nx¯−NΛ(Φ(x¯))dμ⊗N(x)\displaystyle e^{N\delta_{N}}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{N}}\exp\big(N\beta_{N}\bar{x}^{3}+Nh\bar{x}-NI(\bar{x})+\Phi(\bar{x})N\bar{x}-N\Lambda(\Phi(\bar{x})\big)d\mu^{\otimes N}(x)⩽\displaystyle\leqslanteN​δNsupt∈Jηexp(N(βNt3+ht−I(t)))∫ℬNexp(Φ(x¯)Nx¯−NΛ(Φ(x¯))dμ⊗N(x).\displaystyle e^{N\delta_{N}}\sup_{t\in J_{\eta}}\exp\big(N(\beta_{N}t^{3}+ht-I(t))\big)\int_{\mathcal{B}_{N}}\exp\big(\Phi(\bar{x})N\bar{x}-N\Lambda(\Phi(\bar{x})\big)d\mu^{\otimes N}(x).(52)

We next use a discretization ofJηJ_{\eta}. Let𝒫L\mathcal{P}_{L}be a partition ofJηJ_{\eta}into intervals of length at most1L\frac{1}{L}, and for each intervalK∈𝒫LK\in\mathcal{P}_{L}choose a pointtK∈Kt_{K}\in K. The number of intervals is at most2​κ​L2\kappa L. SinceJηJ_{\eta}is compactly contained in(κ−,κ+)(\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}), the functionsΦ\PhiandIIare bounded and Lipschitz onJηJ_{\eta}. Hence, uniformly forxxwithx¯∈K\bar{x}\in K,|(Φ​(x¯)​N​x¯−N​Λ​(Φ​(x¯)))−(Φ​(tK)​N​x¯−N​Λ​(Φ​(tK)))|⩽C​NL,\Big|\Big(\Phi(\bar{x})N\bar{x}-N\Lambda(\Phi(\bar{x}))\Big)-\Big(\Phi(t_{K})N\bar{x}-N\Lambda(\Phi(t_{K}))\Big)\Big|\leqslant\frac{CN}{L},

where the constantCCdepends only onμ,η\mu,\eta.
Using the above display gives∫ℬNexp(Φ(x¯)Nx¯−NΛ(Φ(x¯))dμ⊗N(x)\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{N}}\exp\big(\Phi(\bar{x})N\bar{x}-N\Lambda(\Phi(\bar{x})\big)d\mu^{\otimes N}(x)⩽\displaystyle\leqslanteC​NL∫ℬNexp(Φ(tK)Nx¯−NΛ(Φ(tK))dμ⊗N(x)\displaystyle e^{\frac{CN}{L}}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{N}}\exp\big(\Phi(t_{K})N\bar{x}-N\Lambda(\Phi(t_{K})\big)d\mu^{\otimes N}(x)=\displaystyle=eC​NL​QtK​(ℬN)⩽eC​NL​supt∈JηQt​(ℬN).\displaystyle e^{\frac{CN}{L}}Q_{t_{K}}(\mathcal{B}_{N})\leqslant e^{\frac{CN}{L}}\sup_{t\in J_{\eta}}Q_{t}(\mathcal{B}_{N}).

Combining the above bound along with (4) gives∫ℬNef​(x)​𝑑μ⊗N​(x)⩽eo​(N)+C​NL​supt∈Jηexp⁡(N​βN​t3+N​h​t−N​I​(t))​supt∈JηQt​(ℬN).\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{N}}e^{f(x)}d\mu^{\otimes N}(x)\leqslant e^{o(N)+\frac{CN}{L}}\sup_{t\in J_{\eta}}\exp\big(N\beta_{N}t^{3}+Nht-NI(t)\big)\sup_{t\in J_{\eta}}Q_{t}(\mathcal{B}_{N}).(53)

On the other hand, by part (i) of the lemma,log⁡ZN​(β,h)=supy∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(y)−I​(y))+o​(N).\log Z_{N}(\beta,h)=\sup_{y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)+o(N).

In particular, by restricting the supremum to constant vectorsy=t​𝟏y=t\mathbf{1},ZN​(β,h)⩾exp⁡(supt∈[κ−,κ+](N​βN​t3+N​h​t−N​I​(t))+o​(N)).Z_{N}(\beta,h)\geqslant\exp\Big(\sup_{t\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]}\big(N\beta_{N}t^{3}+Nht-NI(t)\big)+o(N)\Big).(54)

After normalization, (53) and (54) givePβ,h​(X∈ℬN)\displaystyle P_{\beta,h}(X\in\mathcal{B}_{N})=1ZN​(β,h)​∫ℬNef​(x)​𝑑μ⊗N​(x)⩽eo​(N)+C​NL​supt∈JηQt​(ℬN).\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{N}(\beta,h)}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{N}}e^{f(x)}d\mu^{\otimes N}(x)\leqslant e^{o(N)+\frac{CN}{L}}\sup_{t\in J_{\eta}}Q_{t}(\mathcal{B}_{N}).

We first verify the hypothesis of Lemma4.3. SinceΛ′\Lambda^{\prime}is Lipschitz on bounded intervals, forx∈𝒜Nx\in\mathcal{A}_{N},∑i(bi​(x)−b¯​(x))2⩽C​∑i(mi​(x)−m¯​(x))2⩽C​N​εN.\sum_{i}(b_{i}(x)-\bar{b}(x))^{2}\leqslant C\sum_{i}(m_{i}(x)-\bar{m}(x))^{2}\leqslant CN\varepsilon_{N}.

Thus, we haveb​(x)∈ENb(x)\in E_{N}on𝒜N\mathcal{A}_{N}, whereEN:={y∈[κ−,κ+]N:∑i(yi−y¯)2⩽C​εN​N}.E_{N}:=\Big\{y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}:\sum_{i}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}\leqslant C\varepsilon_{N}N\Big\}.

By Lemma3.7,ENE_{N}is of low complexity.

Therefore, the hypothesis (46) of Lemma4.3is satisfied.

It remains to verify the exponentialQtQ_{t}-bound required in Lemma4.3(iii). Letη>0\eta>0be the value supplied by Lemma4.3(iii). Since|ℛj|⩽γ|\mathcal{R}_{j}|\leqslant\gammaby Assumption1.2, we have1N​∑jℛj2⩽γ2.\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j}^{2}\leqslant\gamma^{2}.Therefore, ifTr​(ℛ2)⩾c​N\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{R}^{2})\geqslant cN, then for all sufficiently largeNN,∑i,j(ℛi​j−1N​ℛj)2⩾c2​N.\sum_{i,j}\big(\mathcal{R}_{ij}-\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{R}_{j}\big)^{2}\geqslant\frac{c}{2}N.

uniformly overt∈Jηt\in J_{\eta}. HenceTN​(X)=Ωp​(1)T_{N}(X)=\Omega_{p}(1).

## 5.Mean-Field Analysis

In this section we prove the three mean-field results stated in Section1.
Theorem1.10provides the main mean-field approximation: it identifies the variational limit of the free energy and shows that the conditional mean vectors form a low-complexity family. We then use this structure in Theorem1.11to prove that a variational separation from nearly constant profiles yields macroscopic inhomogeneity of the local fields and hence jointN\sqrt{N}-consistency of the pseudolikelihood estimator.
The section concludes with Theorem1.13, which gives the complementary homogeneous ferromagnetic picture: under regularity and well-connectedness assumptions, the local fields become asymptotically homogeneous and pseudolikelihood estimation becomes ill-conditioned.

## 5.1.Proof of Theorem1.10

Recall thatf​(x)=β​⟨A,x⊗3⟩/3+h​⟨x,𝟏⟩f(x)=\beta\langle A,x^{\otimes 3}\rangle/3+h\langle x,\boldsymbol{1}\rangle.
Let∇f​(x)=(∂1f​(x),…,∂Nf​(x))\nabla f(x)=(\partial_{1}f(x),\ldots,\partial_{N}f(x))be the gradient offf, where∂if​(x)=β​mi​(x)+h\partial_{i}f(x)=\beta m_{i}(x)+h. Set𝒱={∇f​(x):x∈[κ−,κ+]N}⊆ℝN.\mathcal{V}=\big\{\nabla f(x):x\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}\big\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}.

The Gaussian width of a subsetKKofℝN\mathbb{R}^{N}is given by𝖦𝖶​(K)=𝔼​supx∈K⟨x,g⟩,\mathsf{GW}(K)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\sup_{x\in K}\langle x,g\rangle,

whereggis a standard Gaussian vector onℝN.\mathbb{R}^{N}.

A crucial ingredient in our proof is the following mean-field upper bound from[augeri2020nonlinear]for the log-partition function under a general spin distribution, where the error term is controlled by the Gaussian width of the image of∇f\nabla f.

## Theorem 5.1(Corollary 1.2 of[augeri2020nonlinear], reformulated in our case).

There is a numerical constantC>0C>0such thatlog⁡ZN​(β,h)⩽supt∈[κ−,κ+]N(β​⟨A,t⊗3⟩/3+h​⟨t,𝟏⟩−I​(t))+C​κ2/3​N1/3​𝖦𝖶​(𝒱)2/3.\log Z_{N}(\beta,h)\leqslant\sup_{t\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\Big(\beta\langle A,t^{\otimes 3}\rangle/3+h\langle t,\boldsymbol{1}\rangle-I(t)\Big)+C\kappa^{2/3}N^{1/3}\mathsf{GW}(\mathcal{V})^{2/3}.

## Lemma 5.2(Gaussian width bound).

We have𝖦𝖶​(𝒱)⩽2​κ2​|β|​N​log⁡N​‖∑iAi2‖op1/2.\mathsf{GW}(\mathcal{V})\leqslant\sqrt{2}\kappa^{2}|\beta|N\sqrt{\log N}\big\|\sum_{i}A^{2}_{i}\big\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{op}}.

## Proof.

By definition,𝖦𝖶​(𝒱)=𝔼​supx∈[κ−,κ+]N⟨∇f​(x),g⟩=𝔼⁡[h​⟨𝟏,g⟩+supx∈[κ−,κ+]Nβ​∑igi​⟨x,Ai​x⟩].\mathsf{GW}(\mathcal{V})=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\sup_{x\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\langle\nabla f(x),g\rangle=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\Big[h\langle\boldsymbol{1},g\rangle+\sup_{x\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\beta\sum_{i}g_{i}\langle x,A_{i}x\rangle\Big].

Using𝔼⁡⟨𝟏,g⟩=0\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\langle\boldsymbol{1},g\rangle=0, we obtain𝖦𝖶​(𝒱)\displaystyle\mathsf{GW}(\mathcal{V})⩽|β|​𝔼​supx∈[κ−,κ+]N|∑igi​⟨x,Ai​x⟩|⩽|β|​𝔼​sup‖x‖2⩽κ​N|⟨x,∑igi​Ai​x⟩|\displaystyle\leqslant|\beta|\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\sup_{x\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\Big|\sum_{i}g_{i}\langle x,A_{i}x\rangle\Big|\leqslant|\beta|\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\sup_{\|x\|_{2}\leqslant\kappa\sqrt{N}}\Big|\big\langle x,\sum_{i}g_{i}A_{i}x\big\rangle\Big|⩽κ2​|β|​N​𝔼⁡‖∑igi​Ai‖op.\displaystyle\leqslant\kappa^{2}|\beta|N\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\big\|\sum_{i}g_{i}A_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Finally, by the matrix Gaussian series bound (e.g. Theorem 4.1.1 in[tropp2015introduction]),𝔼⁡‖∑igi​Ai‖op⩽2​log⁡N​‖∑iAi2‖op1/2.\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\Big\|\sum_{i}g_{i}A_{i}\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\sqrt{2\log N}\,\Big\|\sum_{i}A_{i}^{2}\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{1/2}.

Combining the last two displays yields the desired bound on𝖦𝖶​(𝒱)\mathsf{GW}(\mathcal{V}).■\blacksquare

For a subsetKKofℝN\mathbb{R}^{N}and for anyδ>0\delta>0, let𝖭​(K,δ)\mathsf{N}(K,\delta)denote the covering number ofKK, that is, the smallest number ofℓ2\ell^{2}-balls inℝN\mathbb{R}^{N}with radiiδ\deltaneeded to coverKK. Note thatK⊆[−1,1]NK\subseteq[-1,1]^{N}is of low complexity, in the sense of Definition1.8, iflog⁡𝖭​(K,δ​N)=o​(N)\log\mathsf{N}(K,\delta\sqrt{N})=o(N)for each fixedδ>0\delta>0.

## Lemma 5.3.

There exists a constantC>0C>0such that for anyδ>0\delta>0and anyK⊆ℝNK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N},𝖭​(K,δ)⩽exp⁡(C​δ−2​𝖦𝖶​(K)2).\mathsf{N}(K,\delta)\leqslant\exp\big(C\delta^{-2}\mathsf{GW}(K)^{2}\big).

## Proof.

Sudakov’s minoration inequality onℝN\mathbb{R}^{N}states that there exists a constantc>0c>0such that for anyK⊆ℝNK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}and for anyδ>0\delta>0,𝖦𝖶​(K)⩾c​δ​log⁡𝖭​(K,δ),\mathsf{GW}(K)\geqslant c\delta\sqrt{\log\mathsf{N}(K,\delta)},

from which the desired upper bound on the covering number ofKKfollows immediately.■\blacksquare

## Proof of Theorem1.10.

Together with Lemma5.3, this implies thatlog⁡𝖭​(𝒱,δ​N)=o​(N)\log\mathsf{N}(\mathcal{V},\delta\sqrt{N})=o(N)for eachδ>0\delta>0. for each fixedδ>0\delta>0. Hence, the setℳN\mathcal{M}_{N}has low complexity.■\blacksquare

Then we haveℙ​(b​(X)∈SN)→0.\mathbb{P}(b({X})\in S_{N})\to 0.

## Proof of Proposition5.4.

By (20), withai=1a_{i}=1, and by (22) of Corollary3.3, together with the mean-field condition (4) and the Lemma3.4, we have𝔼[∑i(Xi−bi(X))]2=o(N2),𝔼[∑i​j​kAi​j​k(XiXjXk−bi(X)bj(X)bk(X))]2=o(N2).\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\Big[\sum_{i}(X_{i}-b_{i}(X))\Big]^{2}=o(N^{2}),\qquad\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\Big[\sum_{ijk}A_{ijk}(X_{i}X_{j}X_{k}-b_{i}(X)b_{j}(X)b_{k}(X))\Big]^{2}=o(N^{2}).

This immediately implies that𝔼(f(X)−f(b(X)))2=o(N2)\operatorname{\mathds{E}}\big(f(X)-f(b(X))\big)^{2}=o(N^{2}). Thus, there exists a sequence of non-negative reals(εN)N⩾1(\varepsilon_{N})_{N\geqslant 1}converging to0, such thatℙ​(|f​(X)−f​(b​(X))|⩽N​εN)→1.\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|f(X)-f(b(X))|\leqslant N\varepsilon_{N})\to 1.

By Theorem1.10(ii), the setℳN:={b​(x):x∈[κ−,κ+]N}\mathcal{M}_{N}:=\{b(x):x\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}\}has low complexity. TakingEN=ℳNE_{N}=\mathcal{M}_{N}, we haveℙ​(b​(X)∈EN)=1\mathbb{P}(b(X)\in E_{N})=1. Therefore the hypothesis (46) of Lemma4.3is satisfied.

Now takeCN=SNC_{N}=S_{N}in Lemma4.3(ii). By the definition ofSNS_{N},supy∈SN(f​(y)−I​(y))⩽supy∈[κ−,κ+]N(f​(y)−I​(y))−δ​N,\sup_{y\in S_{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)\leqslant\sup_{y\in[\kappa_{-},\kappa_{+}]^{N}}\big(f(y)-I(y)\big)-\delta N,

so the variational gap condition in Lemma4.3(ii) holds. Henceℙ​(|f​(X)−f​(b​(X))|⩽N​εN,b​(X)∈SN∩EN)→0.\mathbb{P}\big(|f(X)-f(b(X))|\leqslant N\varepsilon_{N},\ b(X)\in S_{N}\cap E_{N}\big)\to 0.

Since the event{|f​(X)−f​(b​(X))|⩽N​εN,b​(X)∈EN}\{|f(X)-f(b(X))|\leqslant N\varepsilon_{N},\ b(X)\in E_{N}\}has probability tending to one, it follows thatℙ​(b​(X)∈SN)→0\mathbb{P}(b(X)\in S_{N})\to 0.
This proves the proposition.■\blacksquare

We are now ready to prove Theorem1.11.

## Proof of Lemma5.5.

Recall from Section1that𝒫=D−1​ℛ,where​D=diag​(ℛ​(1),…,ℛ​(N)).\mathcal{P}=D^{-1}\mathcal{R},\quad\text{where }D=\mathrm{diag}(\mathcal{R}(1),\ldots,\mathcal{R}(N)).

This is the transition matrix of the random walk on the weighted graph with edge weightsℛi​j\mathcal{R}_{ij}fori≠j∈[N]i\neq j\in[N]. A positive spectral gap ensures that the graph is connected, so𝒫\mathcal{P}is irreducible and there exists a unique stationary distributionπ\pi, given byπ​(i)=ℛiN​ℛ¯,i∈[N].\pi(i)=\frac{\mathcal{R}_{i}}{N\bar{\mathcal{R}}},\ \ i\in[N].

Combining this with (56) applied tof​(i)=zif(i)=z_{i}for eachi∈[N]i\in[N], we obtain12​N​ℛ¯​∑i,jℛi​j​(zi−zj)2⩾δ​(Varν​(z)−o​(1))=δ​(1N​∑i(zi−z¯)2−o​(1))⩾δ2​1N​∑i(zi−z¯)2,\displaystyle\frac{1}{2N\bar{\mathcal{R}}}\sum_{i,j}\mathcal{R}_{ij}(z_{i}-z_{j})^{2}\geqslant\delta\bigl(\text{Var}_{\nu}(z)-o(1)\bigr)=\delta\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}(z_{i}-\bar{z})^{2}-o(1)\bigr)\geqslant\frac{\delta}{2}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}(z_{i}-\bar{z})^{2},
